On the Past Maximal Development of Near-FLRW Data for the Einstein Scalar-Field Vlasov System
Authors: David Fajman, Liam Urban
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We show that the maximal globally hyperbolic development of near-FLRW initial data for the Einstein scalar-field Vlasov system exhibits stable Big Bang formation in the collapsing direction. The solutions exhibit stable Kretschmann scalar blow-up, causing the spacetime to become causally geodesically past incomplete, and are asymptotically velocity term dominated. This is the first stability result for the Einstein equations in the collapsing spacetime direction in presence of Vlasov matter that does not rely on any symmetry assumptions. Furthermore, the Vlasov distribution remains close to that of the FLRW solution as a function on the co-mass shell, and so does its momentum support if one assumes it to be close to that of the FLRW distribution initially. On the other hand, the leading order terms in components of the Vlasov energy-momentum tensor exbihit an offset in asymptotic order controlled by the perturbation size, and when viewed on the mass shell, the distribution asymptotically concentrates in certain preferred velocity directions. To ensure that this behaviour is sufficiently mitigated by the scalar field, we crucially exploit a scaling hierarchy between horizontal and vertical derivatives in the commuted Vlasov equation.

Introduction

Setting and Results

The goal of this paper is to study the past behaviour of the Einstein equations in presence of both scalar field and massive (m=1m=1) or massless (m=0m=0) Vlasov matter near Friedman-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker (FLRW) solutions. We note that, when viewing the Vlasov distribution on the mass shell rather than the co-mass shell, the particle momenta concentrate in directions determined by the leading order asymptotics of the shear, as is discussed in Remark 8.5.

Additionally, for κ >0κ>0, Theorem 1.1 also implies stable Big Crunch formation toward the future: The respective FLRW solutions exist on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{M}=(0,T)\times M$$\end{document}M¯=(0,T)×M for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathbb {R}^+$$\end{document}T∈R+ and are preserved under the time reflection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (0,T)\leadsto T-t$$\end{document}t∈(0,T)⇝T-t (see Lemma 2.1 and 2.2). Thus, the entire analysis toward the Big Bang singularity can be re-applied approaching the Big Crunch singularity in the case of positive spatial curvature.

This is, to our knowledge, the first asymptotic stability result in the contracting direction involving Vlasov matter that does not require any symmetry assumptions. Furthermore, since Vlasov matter asymptotically matches the equation of state of radiation (see Sect. 1.2.2), this extends the regime in which the presence of a scalar field stabilizes Big Bang formation compared to the recent result [BO24b] (see the discussion in Sect. 1.3.2).

Background

Singularity formation and quiescent cosmology

Since the singularity theorems by Penrose [Pen65] and Hawking [Haw67], understanding the nature of singularity formation has been one of the core pursuits of mathematical relativity. While these theorems describe under which conditions spacetimes become causally incomplete, they provide no information about the type of singularity. In cosmological settings, the two following conjectures concerning past singularity formation have garnered particular attention:

Firstly, the Strong Cosmic Censorship (SCC) conjecture states that the maximal development of generic initial data becomes inextendible when it becomes geodesically incomplete. In view of results in the homogeneous setting by Chruściel and Rendall [CR95] and Ringström [Rin00, Rin01]1 as well in Gowdy symmetry by Ringström [Rin09b], the cosmological version of this conjecture usually specifies this to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2-inextendibility caused by blow-up of the Kretschmann scalar. Since directly computing the asymptotic behaviour of the Kretschmann scalar given general initial data is often difficult if not impossible, evidence toward this conjecture is commonly provided by showing that the Kretschmann scalar blow-up of a particular explicit solution is stable within solutions to the Einstein equations. We will discuss the available results in this direction for FLRW and Kasner spacetimes in Sect. 1.3.1.

Secondly, the BKL conjecture (originally formulated in the work of Belinskiǐ, Khalatnikov and Lifshitz [BKL70]) states that the asymptotics of vacuum cosmological solutions to the Einstein equations toward the Big Bang are driven by spatial inhomogeneities via spatial Ricci curvature terms. This should then cause the metric evolution to become highly oscillatory and chaotic, often referred to as “Mixmaster” behaviour (see [Mis69]). Originally, it was conjectured in [BKL70] that introducing matter to the Einstein equations does not change the leading order asymptotics compared the vacuum BKL picture. However, Belinskiǐ and Khalatnikov later observed in [BK73] that scalar field and stiff fluid matter appear to have an exceptional role: These matter models exhibit quiescent Big Bang formation, i.e., this choice of matter dampens any potential oscillations. This generates stable past asymptotics with asymptotically velocity term dominated (AVTD) asymptotics, i.e., the solution agrees at leading order with the asymptotics of the formal system obtained by dropping all spatial derivative terms from the evolution equations and the Hamiltonian constraint. In particular, this would mean that the SCC conjecture holds for such solutions, since the Kretschmann scalar blows up for FLRW solutions with non-trivial scalar field. For a recent comprehensive result on which types of initial data to the Einstein nonlinear scalar field system generate quiescent singularities, see [OGPR26].

Vlasov matter

The universe in its current state can, at large scales, reasonably be modelled as a collisionless self-gravitating gas, where stars are viewed as particles with rest mass m and are distributed according to a distribution function f (see [BT08, Chapters 1.2 and 4.1]). Since one needs to require that all particles of the gas travel along timelike geodesics, f must satisfy the Vlasov equation (1.1e). The asymptotic behaviour of massive Vlasov matter on FLRW spacetimes in particular corresponds very well to the dominant matter components expected in standard cosmological models in the early and late universe (see, for example, [Ryd16, Sections 5.5 and 6.4]): Toward the past, where the universe is believed to have been radiation dominated, the density of massive and massless Vlasov matter behaves like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-4}$$\end{document}a-4 and the equation of state approaches that of a radiation fluid (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}\approx \frac{1}{3}\rho ^{Vl}$$\end{document}pVl≈13ρVl; this holds with equality in the massless case). Toward the future, where one expects matter domination, the pressure converges to 0, approximating the equation of state of dust, and the Vlasov density is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-3}$$\end{document}a-3. Thus, even though a fully physical model of gas toward the Big Bang may need to include collisions, Vlasov matter is already a realistic matter type to consider from the perspective of FLRW cosmology.

While we will discuss some relevant results regarding past existence and asymptotic behaviour in Sect. 1.3, we also refer to [And11] for a more general overview of results regarding the Einstein-Vlasov system.

Relationship with previous work

This paper both expands the known regime of quiescent Big Bang formation caused by scalar fields and, to our knowledge, is the first result on past stability in presence of Vlasov matter without symmetry. Thus, we will proceed as follows: First, we compare our approach with the existing literature on Big Bang formation for the Einstein scalar-field system. Then, we contrast them with results for Einstein scalar-field fluid systems, since modelling the universe as a fluid is a common approach that is closely related to the Vlasov approach (see Sect. 1.2.2). Finally, we compare our results to those for Einstein (scalar-field) Vlasov matter.

Quiescent singularity formation for scalar field matter

Rodnianski and Speck proved the first result [RS18a] regarding past stability of the Einstein scalar-field system without symmetries, showing that Big Bang formation in FLRW spacetimes with spatial geometry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}T3 is linearly stable and asymptotically velocity term dominated, i.e., the spacetime and matter variables asymptotically behave like the solution to the velocity dominated ODE. This was extended to nonlinear stability in [RS18b], and then in turn to spherical and closed hyperbolic spatial hypersurfaces in [Spe18], respectively [FU25]. In the case of flat spatial geometry, the regime of past asymptotic stability has been extended to moderately anisotropic generalized Kasner spacetimes2 in [RS22] and then to the all subcritical3 ones in [FRS23]. That these results can be localized was first shown for spatially flat FLRW spacetimes in [BO24a], and in recent preprints also for the entire subcritical Kasner regime in [BOZ25, Zhe26]. In all of these cases, the Kretschmann scalar exhibits stable blow-up at order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^{-4}$$\end{document}t-4 approaching the Big Bang (t↓ 0t↓0); the solutions are AVTD. Furthermore, Big Bang stability near FLRW spacetimes can be localized, see [BO24a]. These recent developments indicate that spatial geometry has little bearing on the past asymptotic behaviour of non-vacuum solutions to the Einstein scalar-field equations. In fact, in their recent work, Oude Groeniger, Petersen and Ringström [OGPR26] were able to establish a very general criterion for quiescent Big Bang formation in the Einstein non-linear scalar-field system, essentially only requiring the mean curvature to be large enough compared to the initial data and the eigenvalues of the initial Weingarten map are to be sufficiently far from one another (see [OGPR26, Theorem 12]). In terms of Big Bang stability, the latter may be dropped near a wide class of cosmological model solutions, including FLRW spacetimes (see [OGPR26, Theorem 49]).

This independence of spatial geometry is reflected in our result insofar as that we allow space in the reference FLRW spacetime to be of arbitrary constant sectional curvature. To be more precise, the energy formalism we use for the scalar field and spacetime variables extends the approach in [FU25] to FLRW spacetimes with spatial geometry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}T3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^3$$\end{document}S3, and then couples this with an energy mechanism for Vlasov matter. However, restricting ourselves to isotropic reference spacetimes is essential when Vlasov matter is present. Else, both the tracefree part of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{Vl}_{ij}$$\end{document}TijVl and the energy flux no longer vanish in the reference spacetime, and one would likely lose control of the shear and related constrained quantities. Thus, while it might be possible to integrate Vlasov stability analysis into the frameworks of [FRS23] or [OGPR26], which both included anisotropic spacetimes, there seems to be no loss of generality in restricting ourselves to a methodology that only considers the case of an isotropic reference. In particular, we note that one likely cannot use the same approach as in [BO24a] to establish localized stability in the scalar-field Vlasov setting, as this seems bound to run into similar issues as radiation fluids do in [BO24b] (see Sect. 1.3.2).

Past stability in Einstein (scalar-field) Euler models

Given how robust scalar field matter appears to be when considering past asymptotics of cosmological spacetimes, it is a natural next step to analyze whether it can dampen the oscillatory behaviour one would otherwise expect from matter models with AVTD behaviour, as discussed in Sect. 1.2.1.

For example, the dynamics of spatially homogeneous solutions to the Einstein-Euler equations with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_s^2\in (0,1)$$\end{document}cs2∈(0,1) are well understood to asymptote toward vacuum solutions, often of Kasner type, that exhibit anisotropic curvature blow-up (see, for example, [WE97, Rin01, CH03]). Additionally, Beyer and Le Floch analysed homogeneous fluids on fixed (1+d)\,(1+d)—dimensional Kasner backgrounds with linear equation of state \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}=c_s^2\rho $$\end{document}p=cs2ρ in [BL17]. In this setting, the fluid velocity diverges toward the Big Bang in the supercritical case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_s^2<\frac{1}{d}$$\end{document}cs2<1d, is time-independent in the critical case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_s^2=\frac{1}{d}$$\end{document}cs2=1d, and vanishes toward the Big Bang in the subcritical case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\ge c_s^2>\frac{1}{d}$$\end{document}1≥cs2>1d (see [BL17, Theorem 3.3]). In [BL17, Theorems 4.2-4.3], they showed that subcritical and certain critical solutions to the Einstein-Euler system in Gowdy symmetry are velocity dominated, while this can even break down on the Kasner background in the supercritical case (see [BL17, Section 4.3]).

Given the AVTD nature of Big Bang formation in the Einstein scalar-field system, one could thus hope that coupling with subcritical fluids preserves quiescence. Indeed, Beyer and Oliynyk were able to prove in [BO24b] that subcritical FLRW solutions to the Einstein scalar-field Euler system are past stable (see [BO24b, Theorems 3.1 and 3.6]). However, the critical case cannot be controlled by the Fuchsian approach in [BO24b] since leading terms in the evolution of the fluid velocity become indefinite (see [BO24b, Remark 9.2]), which may cause the fluid to degenerate. However, the results of [BL17] indicate that the critical regime may still exhibit stability under stronger assumptions.

Since the critical case corresponds to radiation fluids in spacetime dimension 1+d=41+d=4, and thus to the asymptotic behaviour of Vlasov matter, our results essentially extend the stability regime of [BO24b] to the critical regime for m=0m=0 and slightly beyond for m=1m=1. That FLRW solutions to (1.1) fall into a critical regime is reflected in the fact that only the Vlasov distribution itself remains close to the leading term of its velocity term dominated counterpart, while the natural spatial metric on slices of the CMC foliation cannot be controlled sharply. This causes components of the Vlasov energy momentum tensor to diverge, including the macroscopic pressure and density that one would like to compare to the fluid model. Nevertheless, the solution variables that determine the leading order asymptotics of the spacetime fully retain their AVTD behaviour (see Remark 8.2). Further, that spatial velocity directions cannot be ignored toward the Big Bang is also strongly indicated by even VTD radiation fluids on Kasner backgrounds exhibiting this feature, and that stability of their initial profile can only be ensured under additional symmetry assumptions on the initial data (see [BL17, Theorem 4.3]).

Vlasov matter in cosmological spacetimes

The Einstein-Vlasov system has been studied extensively, especially in the context of future nonlinear stability, while little is known in the contracting regime.

In 2+12+1 gravity, the first author has analyzed the past and future maximal development of cosmological solutions to the Einstein-Vlasov system (see [Faj16, Faj17a, Faj17b, Faj18]). In 3+13+1 dimensions, the system is known to be nonlinearly stable near Minkowski space (see [Tay17, LT20, FJS21, BFJ+21]) and near Milne spacetimes (see [AF20, BF22]). Moreover, future stability is known for the Einstein nonlinear scalar-field massive-Vlasov system with accelerated expansion (see [Rin13]). Most of the tools used therein were extended to the massless setting in [Sve12, Paper B]. The works [Rin13] by Ringström and [Sve12, Paper B] by Svedberg are of particular relevance to this paper, since the local well-posedness results therein can be carried over to our setting. We discuss this in Sect. 3.3.

Regarding the past direction, it was shown in [DR16] that Kretschmann blow-up occurs for any surface-symmetric non-vacuum cosmological solution to the Einstein-Vlasov system, but no statement on the nature of blow-up is made. The first stability result for cosmological solutions to the Einstein-Vlasov system was due to Rein (see [Rei96]) in the same setting; the approach was extended to the scalar-field Vlasov setting by Tegankong, Noutchegueme and Rendall in [TNR04, TR06], as well as to the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \ne 0$$\end{document}Λ≠0 in [Tch04]. In these works, the authors studied solutions of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -e^{-2\mu (a,r)}da^2+e^{2\lambda (a,r)}dr^2+a^2\sigma _{\varepsilon }\, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-e-2μ(a,r)da2+e2λ(a,r)dr2+a2σεand obtained a lower bound on the Kretschmann scalar by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-6}$$\end{document}a-6, ensuring that the Strong Cosmic Censorship conjecture holds in this symmetry class. However, when comparing our results to [Rei96], it should be noted that near-FLRW spacetimes are not covered by the stability result [Rei96, Theorem 5.3], as discussed in the subsequent Remark. Indeed, in these coordinates, FLRW spacetimes correspond to the choices\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda (a,r)=\text {ln}(a),\quad e^{-2\mu (a)}=\frac{8\pi }{3}a^2\rho (a)\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ(a,r)=ln(a),e-2μ(a)=8π3a2ρ(a).Renormalised Vlasov solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\sharp $$\end{document}f♯ on the mass shell depend on the expansion rescaled velocity variables w and F. In [Rei96], the maximum of these quantities on the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\sharp $$\end{document}f♯ at the initial hypersurface a=1a=1 is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_0$$\end{document}w0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_0$$\end{document}F0, respectively. This notation in hand, one computes that FLRW spacetimes satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&10\pi ^2w_0F_0\sqrt{1+w_0^2+F_0}\left\| e^{2\mu (1)}\right\| _{C^0}\left\| f^\sharp _{FLRW}(1)\right\| _{C^0}\\&\quad \ge 5\pi \int _{-w_0}^{w_0}\int _0^{F_0} \sqrt{1+w^2+F}\mathcal {F}(w^2+F)\,dFdw\\&\quad =\,5\pi e^{2\mu (1)}\rho (1)=\frac{15}{8}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}10π2w0F01+w02+F0e2μ(1)C0fFLRW♯(1)C0≥5π∫-w00∫001+w2+FF(w2+F)dFdw=5πe2μ(1)ρ(1)=158.The requirement in [Rei96, Theorems 4.1 and 5.3] that the quantity on the left-hand side is bounded from above by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}12 thus excludes near-FLRW spacetimes from the analysis. There are no such restrictions in the scalar-field Vlasov case studied by Tegankong and Rendall in [TR06] so that the aforementioned lower bound on the Kretschmann scalar still applies to FLRW solutions. This bound is sufficient to show that the Strong Cosmic Censorship conjecture is satisfied for surface symmetric cosmological solutions to (1.1). However, it does not necessarily reflect the leading order asymptotic behaviour of the Kretschmann scalar. Indeed, in presence of a scalar field, the Kretschmann scalar of the FLRW solution blows up at order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-12}$$\end{document}a-12 and for the pure Vlasov case at order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-8}$$\end{document}a-8. In (8.5), we show that this blow-up is stable, i.e., near-FLRW initial data also leads to blow-up at order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-12}$$\end{document}a-12 and the rescaled Kretschmann scalar remains close to its FLRW counterpart. The gap between our work and the lower bounds in [Rei96, TR06] appears since non-negative terms which are dominant near FLRW solutions are dropped in their computations. Moreover, in [TR06, Theorem 3.5], it is shown that the expansion normalized eigenvalues of the second fundamental form converge under rather restrictive assumptions on the behaviour on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\sharp $$\end{document}f♯ along the entire evolution.4 In (8.2e), we show that this is possible for generic near-FLRW data. Thus, our results are not only the first to establish stable past asymptotics without symmetry assumptions, but also refine previous results [Rei96, TR06] in surface symmetry when considering near FLRW spacetimes.

Recently, the preprint [AHS25] by An, He and Shen provided a complementary perspective on quiescient Big Bang formation for the ESFV system. Therein, the authors consider perturbations of strongly subcritical5 generalised Kasner solutions within the Einstein scalar-field massless-Vlasov Maxwell equations. In constrast to our approach, the authors integrate Vlasov matter into the hierarchy of [FRS23] using the continuity equation and appropriate higher order analogues to control components of the Vlasov energy-momentum tensor directly, rather than hierarchically estimating quiescent variables and the Vlasov distribution function. While this method successfully treats a significantly broader class of spacetimes than the present work, said proof currently seems limited to the massless setting and to small Vlasov distributions.

It is also worth noting that neither [DR16] nor [AHS25] make a statement on the asymptotics of the Vlasov distribution itself. In [Rei96, TR06], the velocity support of the Vlasov distribution is contained in arbitrarily small, uniformly shrinking balls around the zero section. By contrast, we show in (8.4a) that, in a weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{10}$$\end{document}C10-space, the Vlasov distribution converges as a function on the co-mass shell to a footprint that is close to the initial distribution. However, one must exhibit some caution in comparing these results, as all of the analysis mentioned above is performed while considering the Vlasov distribution function on the mass shell, while it only converges toward the Big Bang in this work as a function on the co-mass shell. Indeed, when working on the mass shell, particle velocities concentrate in negative eigendirections of the expansion normalised shear \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{Bang}^{\parallel }$$\end{document}KBang‖, as is discussed in Remark 8.5. We leave it open to future work whether any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{Bang}^{\parallel }$$\end{document}KBang‖ close to 0 can be realised given suitable near-FLRW initial data, but draw attention to some works closely related to this question: Since the evolution of the shear is dominated by the scalar field in the ESFV system, it is reasonable to use results in the Einstein scalar-field system for guidance. Since the AVTD asymptotics in these settings are also dominated by the linearized system, we may also consider the linearized Einstein scalar-field equations for (relative) simplicity. In his recent result [Li24], Li established an isomorphism between near-Kasner initial data and data on the Big Bang hypersurface for the linear Einstein scalar-field system. While no nonlinear analogue to this result is currently available, in the preprint [FG24], Franco-Grisales shows that quiescent initial data on the Big Bang hypersurface for the Einstein non-linear scalar field system admit locally Gaussian developments, akin to the analytic setting studied by Andersson and Rendall in [AR01]. This notion of geometric initial data at the singularity differs from that the scattering data of [Li24], instead having been established by Ringström in [Rin25]. In the recent follow-up work [FGR26], Franco-Grisales and Ringström also show that such data is obtained as the asymptotic footprint of initial data as studied in [OGPR26]. In summary, these results all indicate that some form of scattering result is likely to extend the full Einstein scalar-field system, and then the ESFV system. Thus, we conjecture that this concentration of velocities is generic since, if no negative eigendirection exists, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_{Bang}^{\parallel }$$\end{document}KBang‖ must vanish identically. Finally, we note that the co-mass shell perspective has proven useful in tackling past global existence in spatial homogeneity, see [Tch04], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2$$\end{document}T2-symmetry, see [Wea04].

Proof outline

We prove stability via a bootstrap argument on supremum norms of the spacetime metric components, the scalar field and components of the Vlasov energy-momentum-tensor as well as on low order supremum norms of the Vlasov distribution function (see (3.13)). The core argument to improve these assumptions relies on a series of energy estimates (see Definition 3.3), where the first two energy hierarchies adapt the arguments of [FU25] to the current setting, and the third hierarchy provides sufficiently strong bounds on Vlasov energies.

We will first discuss the structural features of the Vlasov equation. Then, we discuss how the argument of [FU25] is adapted to include Vlasov matter. We refer to [FU25, Section 1.4.1] for a more detailed discussion of the bootstrap mechanism and the energy hierarchy for the spacetime and the scalar field variables.

Features of Vlasov matter toward the Big Bang

By viewing the Vlasov distribution as a function on the co-mass shell, the Vlasov equation admits a particularly simple form in CMCTC gauge (see (2.20a)). In particular, momentum characteristics remain uniformly bounded throughout the analysis if the momentum support is bounded initially. However, this structure becomes significantly more complicated once one commutes the equation with covariant derivatives adapted to this foliation. Firstly, one generates terms of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^{-1}$$\end{document}t-1 containing the shear which, a priori, prevent us from obtaining integrable estimates. This essentially means that shear terms reappear that one would also have at order zero in the mass shell formulation, see (3.12). Secondly, even if one were to use a different commutation method, (2.20a) still contains high order lapse terms that can only be estimated at the cost of scaling and are thus effectively borderline on an energy level. To obtain closed estimates, we leverage the internal structure of the commuted Vlasov equation in, to our knowledge, a novel manner: Toward the past, terms containing momentum derivatives (“vertical derivatives”) of f may diverge faster than those with derivatives orthogonal to the momentum (“horizontal derivatives”). However, such terms either occur nonlinearly and thus inherit smallness from lower order a priori estimates (see Lemma 4.2), or are linear high order shear and lapse terms arising due to the reference distribution. In particular, when commuting the Vlasov equation with, for example, K horizontal and L-KL-K vertical derivatives, those terms with more than K horizontal derivatives remain integrable in time toward the Big Bang even when the Vlasov distribution function itself can only be controlled imprecisely (e.g., using the bootstrap assumption). This allows us to obtain improved estimates by iterating over increasing K, given one takes care regarding the coupling with lapse and shear terms as discussed below.

We remark that the horizontal derivative as it occurs in the Vlasov equation involves Christoffel symbols of the evolved metric and is thus not necessarily globally defined. While low order supremum bounds can essentially be proven locally, energy estimates can only be obtained globally. However, using the blockdiagonal structure of suitably rescaled Sasaki metrics on the cotangent bundle, along with the associated covariant derivatives, one can still contract over the subspace of vertical derivatives and its complement (see Remark 2.4), which allows one to globally define energies that only measure a fixed number of horizontal derivatives of f. This is crucial as it allows us to exploit this scaling hierarchy within our global energy formalism.

Integrating Vlasov matter into the scalar field stability mechanism

Initial data and the bootstrap argument. We may take initial data to be CMC without loss of generality, and are thus able to work in CMC gauge with zero shift. Furthermore, we assume that the solution variables are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^2$$\end{document}ε2-close to their FLRW counterparts in Sobolev spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^\ell $$\end{document}Hℓ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \le 19$$\end{document}ℓ≤19, with their norms collected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{top}$$\end{document}Htop, as well as in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{m}$$\end{document}Cm with młe 16m≤16, collected in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C, see (2.20a). These spaces are defined with respect to the contraction normalized spatial metric G (see (2.9a)) or, for the Vlasov distribution function, the rescaled Sasaki metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{G}_0$$\end{document}G_0 (see (2.18)).

On the bootstrap interval, we first assume\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}\lesssim \varepsilon a^{-c\sigma } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C≲εa-cσfor c>0c>0 and some sufficiently small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \gg \varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}$$\end{document}σ≫ε18. This small divergent factor is necessary due to the spatial metric in particular degenerating toward the Big Bang singularity. Our goal is to improve this bound to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}\lesssim \varepsilon ^\frac{7}{4}a^{-c^\prime \varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C≲ε74a-c′ε18for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^\prime >0$$\end{document}c′>0 by establishing improved energy bounds. We then extend these to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H with conditional coercivity estimates and then to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C using Sobolev embeddings with respect to γ γ. Regarding Vlasov matter, Lemma 4.14 provides control on the energy-momentum components based on specific Vlasov energies, which allows us to control the Vlasov matter quantities in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C directly upon Sobolev embedding with respect to γ γ. While we may still obtain strong Sobolev bounds for the Vlasov distribution function using similar conditional coercivity bounds with respect to the Sasaki metric, this is not required for the bootstrap argument. Additionally, we assume a weaker bound on low order derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f-f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}f-fFLRW that are not exclusively vertical, see (3.13b). This is only needed to obtain a priori bounds on the Vlasov distribution function which then also improve upon this assumption.

That the bootstrap assumptions hold on an initial interval in the chosen gauge is ensured by the local well-posedness result in Lemma 3.9. To establish this, we take the local existence, continuation criteria and Cauchy stability results in harmonic gauge from [Rin13] in the massive and [Sve12, Paper B] in the massless case. From there, we argue as in [Sha11, RS18b] to extend the result from harmonic gauge to CMC gauge, where the continuation criteria need to be adapted accordingly.

A priori bounds. To deal with nonlinear terms in the evolution equations, we need improved bounds on the solution variables at low order. In the FLRW picture, the Vlasov matter quantities are dominated by the scalar field. More precisely, the energy density and pressure of Vlasov matter behave like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-4}$$\end{document}a-4 while their scalar field counterparts behave like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-6}$$\end{document}a-6. This is preserved by the bootstrap assumptions up to a factor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{\pm c\sigma }$$\end{document}a±cσ. Meanwhile, the respective energy fluxes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{0i}^{SF}$$\end{document}T0iSF and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{0i}^{Vl}$$\end{document}T0iVl vanish for the FLRW spacetime, and their perturbed counterparts can both only be bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon a^{-3-c\sigma }$$\end{document}εa-3-cσ. Since, however, the energy flux only occurs in the momentum constraint equation, which is not used to establish a priori bounds, low order improvements for shear, Bel-Robinson, metric and scalar field variables are then obtained as in [FU25, Section 4], where the Vlasov terms are always asymptotically negligible, see Lemma 4.1f..

As for the Vlasov distribution itself, the distribution function at order zero and the evolution of its support can be dealt with by applying the method of characteristics to the rescaled Vlasov equation (2.20a). The commuted equations at order L cause borderline error terms of the same order. As outlined in Sect. 1.4.1, these borderline terms can only add vertical derivatives and are nonlinear, so that the other low order bounds can be applied. Thus, one still obtains a priori estimates for L vertical derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f-f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}f-fFLRW along similar lines as at order zero, using the low order Vlasov bootstrap assumption on the horizontal derivatives. This can be repeated over increasing order of horizontal derivatives, where all borderline terms contain fewer horizontal derivatives and hence are already improved. In the case where all derivatives are horizontal, the bootstrap assumption is not required, yielding a stronger improvement than previously obtained for the mixed terms. Successively replacing the bootstrap assumption with this improved bound then yields sufficiently strong a priori bounds on the distribution function that, in particular, improve (3.13b).

Energy estimates. Regarding Vlasov energies, the transport equation for the distribution function f yields a mechanism for energy near-conservation using integration by parts (see Lemma 6.2). We then use Sasaki-commuted Vlasov equations to obtain high order energy estimates. At these higher orders, we again need to deal with borderline error terms: Considering Vlasov terms with K horizontal and L-KL-K vertical derivatives, differentiating in time and applying the Vlasov equation, commuting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t$$\end{document}∂t with horizontal Sasaki derivatives leads to error terms of the form1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a^{-3}*\nabla \Sigma *|\underline{\nabla }^{L-K}_{vert}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^{K-1}(f-f_{FLRW})|_{\underline{G}_0}^2\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a-3∗∇Σ∗|∇_vertL-K∇_horK-1(f-fFLRW)|G_02.At first glance, these diverge too strongly for the argument to close, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \Sigma $$\end{document}∇Σ can only be bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}$$\end{document}εa-cε using the a priori bound (4.3b). However, as for the a priori estimates, such terms always contain less horizontal derivatives than the terms they originate from. Moreover, they do not occur in the purely vertical case. Thus, we can obtain a suitable energy estimate for the case of only having vertical derivatives, up to a term of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _t^{t_0}a(s)^{-1-c\sigma -\omega }\cdot a(s)^\omega \mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,1}(f,s)\,ds\, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫t0a(s)-1-cσ-ω·a(s)ωE1,1(L)(f,s)dsfor some fixed ω >0ω>0. Similarly, we obtain a series of hierarchized energy estimates for mixed and purely horizontal Vlasov energies, where energies with K horizontal derivatives are scaled by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{(K+1)\omega }$$\end{document}a(K+1)ω. Said scaling means that borderline terms such as (1.3) are scaled more strongly than the energies they are controlled by, and thus do not enter the energy estimates as borderline terms. This can be combined to a total Vlasov energy estimate on the sum of all Vlasov energies at order L with their respective scaling, see Lemma 7.3.

Additionally, we note that the Sasaki metric contains first order derivatives of the spatial metric in its components, and the Vlasov equation also contains first order derivatives. Hence, it is not obvious that our commutation method does not lose regularity. In fact, to ensure that high order metric errors arising from commutator errors are kept under control, we use scaled curvature energy estimates, see (5.19d) and (5.19g).

To ultimately improve the bootstrap assumptions on Vlasov matter quantities, these scaled energy estimates are insufficient. To complete the argument, we derive a complementary, unscaled series of estimates with the same hierarchical approach. Regarding terms of the type described in (1.3), the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \Sigma $$\end{document}∇Σ is small, albeit divergent, ensures that no loss of \varepsilon ε-control occurs as soon as we allow for horizontal derivatives. Otherwise, these energy estimates are proven in much the same way as their scaled counterparts. Since they are slightly more delicate, we always focus on proving the latter type in Sect. 6 and sketch the relevant adaptations for the former scaled estimates.

Energy estimates for spacetime quantities and scalar field matter follow along similar lines as in [FU25], since Vlasov energies enter the respective estimates with time-integrable weights, as we discuss in Sect. 5.2. In particular, since the sectional curvature is negligible compared to both matter contributions in the Friedman equation (2.3a) and we do not make use of the specific value of sectional curvature otherwise, extending the argument of [FU25] to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}κ∈R also only leads to error terms of low order (see, in particular, Lemma 7.1). When considering how Vlasov matter couples into the remaining field equations, the rescaled momentum constraint (2.21i) is the only term that is not evidently asymptotically weaker than scalar field matter. Here, the leading scalar field term is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ca^{-3}\nabla \phi $$\end{document}Ca-3∇ϕ. Note that scalar field energies scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \phi $$\end{document}∇ϕ by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^4$$\end{document}a4. Accounting for this, Vlasov energies always enter spacetime and scalar field energy estimates with time integrable weights that are strictly smaller than those for scalar field energies. In anticipation of the total energy discussed next paragraph, we also derive energy estimates scaled by powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\frac{\omega }{4}$$\end{document}aω4 for the shear, for Bel-Robinson variables and curvature terms, which follow by similar arguments as their unscaled counterparts.

Bootstrap improvement and the main theorem. Structurally, our strategy to obtain strong energy bounds is to construct estimates of the following schematic form:1.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}(t)\lesssim \,\mathcal {F}(t_0)&+\int _t^{t_0}\left( \varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8} s^{-1}+\langle \text {time-integrable terms}\rangle \right) \mathcal {F}(s)\,ds\nonumber \\&+\langle \text {already improved terms}\rangle \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(t)≲F(t0)+∫t0ε18s-1+⟨time-integrable terms⟩F(s)ds+⟨already improved terms⟩.As the first step in this procedure, we slightly oversimplify the total energy, see Definition 7.4, into the caricature\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {E}_{total}=\mathfrak {E}_{total,SF}+\left( \varepsilon ^\frac{1}{4}+a^\frac{\omega }{4}\right) \mathfrak {E}_{total,metric}+\mathfrak {E}_{total,Vl}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Etotal=Etotal,SF+ε14+aω4Etotal,metric+Etotal,Vl.Here, the contribution\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {E}_{quiesc}=\mathfrak {E}_{total,SF}+\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{4}\mathfrak {E}_{total,metric} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Equiesc=Etotal,SF+ε14Etotal,metriccorresponds to the main terms of the total energy for the Einstein scalar-field system as in [FU25], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}_{total,Vl}$$\end{document}Etotal,Vl is a hierarchical combination of Vlasov energies as outlined above, where any individual term is scaled by at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{\omega }$$\end{document}aω. This leads to the leading order scalar field and metric terms entering from Vlasov energy estimates to be bounded by1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _t^{t_0}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+\frac{\omega }{8}}\cdot \left( \mathfrak {E}_{total,SF}(s)+a(s)^\frac{\omega }{4}\mathfrak {E}_{total,metric}(s)\right) \,ds\, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫t0a(s)-3-cε+ω8·Etotal,SF(s)+a(s)ω4Etotal,metric(s)dsup to a multiplicative constant. In other words, if we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \gg \sigma >0$$\end{document}ω≫σ>0, this is compatible with (1.4) applied to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}_{total}$$\end{document}Etotal since the prefactor in (1.5) is integrable in time, see Lemma 2.2. On the other hand, Vlasov energies at worst couple into the estimates of the remaining energies as1.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _t^{t_0}a(s)^{-2-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }-20\,\omega } \mathfrak {E}_{total,Vl}(s)\,ds\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫t0a(s)-2-cε-20ωEtotal,Vl(s)ds.Consequently, this prefactor can be kept time-integrable if one chooses ω ω to be sufficiently small, while still significantly larger than σ σ. In summary, this yields an estimate of the form (1.4) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}_{total}$$\end{document}Etotal and consequently to the strong bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}_{total}\lesssim \varepsilon ^4a^{-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}$$\end{document}Etotal≲ε4a-cε18, see Proposition 7.6.

While this bound is sufficient to improve solely the \varepsilon ε-prefactor in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C, it would only show that geometric and Vlasov matter energies diverge at rates no worse than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-c\omega }$$\end{document}a-cω for some c>0c>0, which is insufficient to improve the boostrap assumption since ω ω must be sufficiently larger than σ σ. However, this bound is still strong enough to ensure that (1.6) can be bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^4$$\end{document}ε4 up to multiplicative constant. Thus, this term is already sufficiently improved, and we can essentially reapply the same schematic argument to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}_{quiesc}$$\end{document}Equiesc as to the total energy in [FU25] to obtain an estimate of the form (1.4). This leads to strong bounds on all components of the spacetime metric, see Proposition 7.7 and Corollary 7.8. To obtain strong Vlasov energy bounds (see Proposition 7.9), we then iterate the unscaled Vlasov energy estimates over horizontal and total derivative orders, using that the other energy quantities are already improved to obtain a series of inequalities of the schematic form (1.4).

The energy bounds then yield an improvement of the bootstrap assumption (3.13a) as outlined at the start of this subsection, closing the bootstrap argument. Most parts of the main theorem, including geodesic incompleteness and curvature blow-up stability, now follow from these improved bounds just as in [RS18b, Theorem 15.1] or [FU25, Theorem 8.2]. In particular, one can perform a similar argument for the Vlasov distribution itself, though one loses one derivative order in doing so since one needs to directly estimate spatial and momentum derivatives of the distribution that appear in the Vlasov equation.

Structure of the paper

In Sect. 2, we introduce notation and the decomposed Einstein equations as well as some useful formulas. Section 3 collects the necessary solution norms to phrase the initial data and bootstrap assumptions, and we establish a local well-posedness result therein along with continuaton criteria for solutions to the ESFV system. In Sect. 4, we prove a priori bounds and estimates for the solution variables based on the bootstrap assumptions, including low order supremum norm bounds and some near-norm equivalencies. Energy estimates for spacetime and scalar field variables are collected in Sect. 5, and the energy estimates for Vlasov matter in Sect. 6. All of these energy estimates are combined in Sect. 7, which is then used to prove Big Bang stability in Sect. 8.

Preliminaries

Notation

Foliation, CMC gauge and spacetime objects

In this paper, we study the Einstein equations in CMC gauge with zero shift unless stated otherwise. Our (3+1)(3+1)-dimensional spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{M},\overline{g})$$\end{document}(M¯,g¯) is thus foliated by constant mean curvature Cauchy hypersurfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{s}=t^{-1}(\{s\})$$\end{document}Ms=t-1({s}) for a time function t. These hypersurfaces are diffeomorphic to the closed orientable 3-manifold M, and thus we will often replace \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_s$$\end{document}Ms by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{s\}\times M$$\end{document}{s}×M or simply M without further comment. With respect to this foliation, the spacetime metric is written as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{g}=-n^2dt^2+g_{ab}dx^adx^b\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g¯=-n2dt2+gabdxadxb.We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\xi (t)}^{a_1\dots a_r}}_{b_1\dots b_r}$$\end{document}ξ(t)a1⋯arb1⋯br for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{g}$$\end{document}g¯-orthogonal projection of any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_t$$\end{document}Mt-tangent tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\xi ^{\alpha _1\dots \alpha _r}}_{\beta _1\dots \beta _s}$$\end{document}ξα1⋯αrβ1⋯βs onto the hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_t$$\end{document}Mt. Indices

We apply the following index conventions:Lowercase Latin indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b,\dots ,i,j,\dots $$\end{document}a,b,⋯,i,j,⋯ run from 1 to 3. In the context of spatial derivatives or components of spatial tensors, these are always with respect to an arbitrary local coordinate system \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x^i)_{i=1,2,3}$$\end{document}(xi)i=1,2,3 on M.Lowercase Greek indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta ,\dots ,\mu ,\nu ,\dots $$\end{document}α,β,⋯,μ,ν,⋯ are spacetime indices and run from 0 to 3. Unless stated otherwise, the index 0 denotes components relative to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _0$$\end{document}∂0, and indices 1, 2, 3 denote spatial indices as discussed above.Underlined uppercase Latin letters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{I},\underline{J},\dots $$\end{document}I_,J_,⋯ index objects on the cotangent bundle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M$$\end{document}T∗M (or, respectively, the co-mass shell P, see Sect. 2.5) and run from 1 to 6. Here, indices 1 to 3 refer to spatial coordinates and horizontal directions, while indices 4 to 6 refer to momentum coordinates and vertical directions (also see Sect. 2.5). In this spirit, we occasionally denote the horizontal components of this frame by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{i},\underline{j},\dots $$\end{document}i_,j_,⋯ and the vertical components of this frame by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{i+3},\underline{j+3},\dots $$\end{document}i+3_,j+3_,⋯. Metric conventions and notation

For any Semi-Riemannian metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {g}$$\end{document}g, its Levi-Civita connection is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla [\mathfrak {g}]$$\end{document}∇[g], and the Riemannian curvature tensor is defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla [\mathfrak {g}]_\alpha \nabla [\mathfrak {g}]_\beta Z_\gamma -\nabla [\mathfrak {g}]_{\beta } \nabla [\mathfrak {g}]_{\alpha }Z_\gamma ={\text { {Riem}}[\mathfrak {g}]_{\alpha \beta \gamma }}^\delta Z_\delta \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇[g]α∇[g]βZγ-∇[g]β∇[g]αZγ=Riem[g]αβγδZδ.The Levi-Civita tensor is written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\varepsilon }[\mathfrak {g}]$$\end{document}ε[g]. Further, the respective Japanese bracket is defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \,\cdot \,\rangle _\mathfrak {g}=\sqrt{1+|\,\cdot \,|_\mathfrak {g}^2}$$\end{document}⟨·⟩g=1+|·|g2. Constants

For scalar functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1,\zeta _2$$\end{document}ζ1,ζ2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _2\ge 0$$\end{document}ζ2≥0, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1\lesssim \zeta _2$$\end{document}ζ1≲ζ2 if and only if there exists a constant K>0K>0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\max \left( \zeta _1,0\right) \le K\zeta _2$$\end{document}maxζ1,0≤Kζ2, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1\simeq \zeta _2$$\end{document}ζ1≃ζ2 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1,\zeta _2\ge 0$$\end{document}ζ1,ζ2≥0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _1\lesssim \zeta _2$$\end{document}ζ1≲ζ2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _2\lesssim \zeta _1$$\end{document}ζ2≲ζ1. Implicit constants are allowed to depend on data from the FLRW reference solution as well as the initial time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0$$\end{document}t0 and abstract algebraic constants we may introduce over the course of the argument. In particular, these constants never depend on the initial data parameter \varepsilon >0ε>0, see Assumption 3.4, or the bootstrap parameter σ >0σ>0, see Assumption 3.11, to ensure that suitably small choices of these parameters allow the bootstrap argument to close. We also already note that the same rules for implicit constants apply throughout the bootstrap argument to updates of the abstract constant c>0c>0 as it appears in the bootstrap assumption; we refer to Sect. 3.4 for further details.

The background solution and the Friedman equation

We collect properties of the FLRW reference solution:

Lemma 2.1

(FLRW solutions to the ESFV system). Consider an FLRW spacetime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{M},\overline{g}_{FLRW})$$\end{document}(M¯,g¯FLRW) of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\overline{M}=(0,T)\times M,\ \overline{g}_{FLRW}=-dt^2+a(t)^2\gamma ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(M¯=(0,T)×M,g¯FLRW=-dt2+a(t)2γ)for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathbb {R}^+\cup \{\infty \}$$\end{document}T∈R+∪{∞}, a closed orientable 3-manifold (M,γ )(M,γ) of constant sectional curvature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}κ∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in C^\infty (0,\infty )$$\end{document}a∈C∞(0,∞). Further, consider isotropic scalar field matter and Vlasov matter, i.e., 2.2a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t{\phi }_{FLRW}=C a(t)^{-3},\quad \nabla {\phi }_{FLRW}=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tϕFLRW=Ca(t)-3,∇ϕFLRW=0,2.2b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {f}_{FLRW}(t,x,p_i)={\mathcal {F}}\left( (\gamma ^{-1})^{ij}p_ip_j\right) ,\quad {\mathcal {F}}\in C_c^\infty (\mathbb {R}^+_0,\mathbb {R}^+_0) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fFLRW(t,x,pi)=F(γ-1)ijpipj,F∈Cc∞(R0+,R0+)and2.2c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl}(t)=&\,4\pi \int _0^\infty R^2\sqrt{m^2a(t)^2+R^2} \mathcal {F}(R^2)dR\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρFLRWVl(t)=4π∫0∞R2m2a(t)2+R2F(R2)dR,2.2d\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathfrak {p}}_{FLRW}^{Vl}(t)=&\,\frac{4\pi }{3}\int _0^\infty \frac{R^4}{\sqrt{m^2a(t)^2+R^2}}\mathcal {F}(R^2)dR\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pFLRWVl(t)=4π3∫0∞R4m2a(t)2+R2F(R2)dR.

Proof

This is a standard computation, see [O’N83, p. 345] . □ □

Given the Friedman equations (2.3), the mean curvature (see (2.1)) satisfies the following identities: 2.4a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau ^2=&\,12\pi C^2a^{-6}+24\pi a^{-4}{\rho }^{Vl}_{FLRW}-9\kappa a^{-2}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τ2=12πC2a-6+24πa-4ρFLRWVl-9κa-2,2.4b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t\tau =&\,12\pi C^2a^{-6}+12\pi a^{-4}\left( {\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl}+{\mathfrak {p}}_{FLRW}^{Vl}\right) -3\kappa a^{-2}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tτ=12πC2a-6+12πa-4ρFLRWVl+pFLRWVl-3κa-2. Since the scalar field remains the leading term in (2.3a) approaching a=0a=0, the asymptotic behaviour of the scale factor toward the Big Bang singularity is not affected by Vlasov matter or the sectional curvature. More precisely, it satisfies the following properties.

Lemma 2.2

(Scale factor analysis). Let a be a solution to (2.3a) for C>0C>0 with a(0)=0a(0)=0 that is positive somewhere.
