Proof

For the first statement, we note that the functions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a\mapsto \int _0^\infty R^2\sqrt{m^2a^2+R^2}\mathcal {F}(R^2)dR,\ a\mapsto \int _0^\infty \frac{R^4}{\sqrt{m^2a^2+R^2}}\mathcal {F}(R^2)dR \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a↦∫0∞R2m2a2+R2F(R2)dR,a↦∫0∞R4m2a2+R2F(R2)dRare analytic on (0,∞ )(0,∞) since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}$$\end{document}F is compactly supported. Thus, (2.3a) is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{a}=G(a)$$\end{document}a˙=G(a), where G is analytic on (0,∞ )(0,∞). The case of κ >0κ>0 follows as in the proof of [Spe18, Lemma 3.5]. (2.6)–(2.7) are proven as in [FU25, Lemma 2.4] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \le 0$$\end{document}κ≤0 and [Spe18, Lemma 3.7] for κ >0κ>0. (2.5) follows immediately from (2.3a). □ □

Below, in the case where κ >0κ>0, we will always tacitly assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0\le \frac{T}{2}$$\end{document}t0≤T2, i.e., we take our initial data during the era in which the universe does not contract. By Lemma 3.6, we may do so without loss of generality. By reflection at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{T}{2}$$\end{document}T2, all of our statements regarding Big Bang stability in this case also apply identically to stability of the Big Crunch as t↑ Tt↑T.

Sasaki metrics

In this section, we review properties of the Sasaki metric and the resulting geometry on the cotangent bundle that will underpin our analysis of the Vlasov equation. For a more detailed overview, we refer to [AnCGS22, Section 2.4].

The rescaled metric G on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_t$$\end{document}Mt induces the Sasaki metric on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M_t$$\end{document}T∗Mt by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{G}=G_{ij}dx^idx^j+(G^{-1})^{ij}Dv_iDv_j\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G_=Gijdxidxj+(G-1)ijDviDvj,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Dv_i=dv_i-\Gamma [G]^j_{ik}v_jdx^k$$\end{document}Dvi=dvi-Γ[G]ikjvjdxk. Remark 2.4

(Notation for horizontal and vertical covariant derivatives) A technical difficulty arises from the fact that the horizontal frame \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\textbf{A}_i\}_{i=1,2,3}$$\end{document}{Ai}i=1,2,3 is not globally defined. On the other hand, it naturally arises in the Vlasov equation (2.20a), and our hierarchy will require us to treat horizontal and vertical covariant derivatives differently. One can, however, split vector fields on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M_t$$\end{document}T∗Mt into their horizontal and vertical components globally.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{T^*M}:T^*M\rightarrow M$$\end{document}πT∗M:T∗M→M denote the canonical projection and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\pi _{T^*M}: TT^*M\rightarrow T^*M$$\end{document}TπT∗M:TT∗M→T∗M its tangent map. Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker {T\pi _{T^*M}}$$\end{document}kerTπT∗M defines the vertical cotangent bundle. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {V}(T^*M)$$\end{document}V(T∗M) be the space of sections of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$TT^*M$$\end{document}TT∗M over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M$$\end{document}T∗M which are valued in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker {T\pi _{T^*M}}$$\end{document}kerTπT∗M. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {V}(T^*M)$$\end{document}V(T∗M) is spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\textbf{B}^1,\textbf{B}^2,\textbf{B}^3\}$$\end{document}{B1,B2,B3}. Its orthogonal complement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {V}(T^*M)^\perp $$\end{document}V(T∗M)⊥ admits the local basis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\textbf{A}_1,\textbf{A}_2,\textbf{A}_3\}$$\end{document}{A1,A2,A3}. We now split vector fields using the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{G}$$\end{document}G_-orthogonal projection\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}: \mathfrak {X}(TT^*M)\longrightarrow \mathfrak {V}(T^*M),\ \mathbb {P}W=\sum _{i=1,2,3}\underline{G}(\textbf{B}^i,W)\textbf{B}^i\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P:X(TT∗M)⟶V(T∗M),PW=∑i=1,2,3G_(Bi,W)Bi,onto vertical vector fields. Covariant derivatives can now also be split into horizontal and vertical components by taking\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{\nabla }_{vert}: \mathbb {P}(\mathfrak {X}(TT^*M))\times \mathfrak {X}(TT^*M)\longrightarrow \mathfrak {X}(TT^*M),\ (\underline{\nabla }_{vert})_{\mathbb {P}(W)}Z=\underline{\nabla }_{\mathbb {P}(W)}Z \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇_vert:P(X(TT∗M))×X(TT∗M)⟶X(TT∗M),(∇_vert)P(W)Z=∇_P(W)Zand analogously for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{hor}$$\end{document}∇_hor replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}$$\end{document}P with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}^\perp =1-\mathbb {P}$$\end{document}P⊥=1-P. For the sake of simplicity, we will often view \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{vert}$$\end{document}∇_vert as an operator defined on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {V}(T^*M)\times \mathfrak {X}(TT^*M)$$\end{document}V(T∗M)×X(TT∗M), i.e., as only applying vertical covariant derivatives, and analogously view \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{hor}$$\end{document}∇_hor. Remark 2.5

(Lifting to the mass co-shell) In the following, we will need \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }$$\end{document}∇_ (and the derived connections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{vert}$$\end{document}∇_vert and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{hor}$$\end{document}∇_hor as well as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\nabla }}\equiv \nabla [\underline{\gamma }]$$\end{document}∇_^≡∇[γ_]) to act on quantities that are not a priori defined on the co-mass shell and thus, technically, need to be lifted to it. We will suppress such lifts throughout the paper: For example, when considering any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_t$$\end{document}Mt-tangent tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {T}$$\end{document}T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }\mathfrak {T}$$\end{document}∇_T will refer to the covariant Sasaki derivative on the pullback of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {T}$$\end{document}T along the canonical projection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _t:P_t\simeq T^*M_t\rightarrow M_t$$\end{document}πt:Pt≃T∗Mt→Mt. In particular, one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{{i}}\mathfrak {T}=\nabla _i\mathfrak {T},\quad \underline{\nabla }^{i+3}\mathfrak {T}=0\,.$$\end{document}∇_iT=∇iT,∇_i+3T=0.Conversely, we lift the momentum variables v to the covector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{v}$$\end{document}v~ on the co-mass shell at time t by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{v}_i=0,\quad \tilde{v}_{i+3}=v_i\,,$$\end{document}v~i=0,v~i+3=vi,and simply write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }v_i:=\underline{\nabla }\tilde{v}_{i+3}$$\end{document}∇_vi:=∇_v~i+3. We collect various derivatives of momentum variables in Lemma 2.7. We write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^{\sharp i}=(\underline{G}\vert _{vert}^{-1})^{ij}v_j=(G^{-1})^{ij}v_j$$\end{document}v♯i=(G_|vert-1)ijvj=(G-1)ijvj and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }v^\sharp $$\end{document}∇_v♯ analogously, as well as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|v|_G$$\end{document}|v|G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|v|_\gamma $$\end{document}|v|γ.

3+13+1-decomposition of the Einstein equations

Miscellaneous formulas

To finish this preliminary section, we collect some useful commutator and derivative formulas which can all be proven by straightforward computation:

Lemma 2.6

(Commuting derivatives and integrals). Proof

Norm Setup, Local Well-Posedness and Bootstrap Assumptions

Norms and energies

In this section, we introduce the necessary norms to state our initial data and bootstrap assumptions, as well as the energies we will utilise in our bootstrap improvement mechanism.

Definition 3.1

(Norms and function spaces). The following combined solution norms will be used to efficiently encode the initial data and bootstrap assumptions:

We note that we do not include the highest order lapse norm that we could control in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C: As one can see from Sect. 5.1, we can in fact control lapse energies up to order 21, but at high orders, these scaled energy estimates become progressively weaker. Conversely, for the bootstrap assumption, we only need to assume low order lapse bounds to control nonlinear error terms, and in particular will need to use that the lapse converges to 1 near the Big Bang hypersurface. Thus, there is no benefit in including higher order lapse norms in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C for the bootstrap assumption. In fact, including the lapse in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H is redundant due to the elliptic estimates in Sect. 5.1, and we only include it for the sake of convenience. To improve the bootstrap assumptions, we will make use of the following energies:

Initial data assumptions

Using the solution norms introduced above, we can compactly state our initial data assumption as follows:

Assumption 3.4

(Initial data assumptions). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M,\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\pi },\mathring{\psi },\mathring{f})$$\end{document}(M,g˚,k˚,π˚,ψ˚,f˚) be CMC initial data to the ESFV system as introduced in Sect. 2.3. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{f}\ge 0$$\end{document}f˚≥0 is assumed to have compact momentum support, and in the case of massless Vlasov matter, we additionally assume that f vanishes on an open neighbourhood of the zero section of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M$$\end{document}T∗M.

For some sufficiently small \varepsilon >0ε>0 and some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0>0$$\end{document}t0>0, we assume3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {H}(t_0)+\mathcal {H}_{top}(t_0)+\mathcal {C}(t_0)\le \varepsilon ^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(t0)+Htop(t0)+C(t0)≤ε2as well as3.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^{11}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_{t_0})}\le \varepsilon ^2\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C1,G_011(T∗Mt0)≤ε2.

Local well-posedness

For all that follows, we need to establish that solutions to the ESFV system (1.1) locally exist in CMC gauge with zero shift, that all solution norms and energies are sufficiently regular in time and that we can extend solutions as long as we can control the size of our variables sufficiently well. To this end, we first collect local well-posedness results in harmonic gauge from [Rin13, Sve12], and then sketch how their proofs can be combined with results for the Einstein stiff-fluid system in CMC gauge to obtain the result for the ESFV system. In particular, we also collect a Cauchy stability result in harmonic gauge to argue that restricting the analysis to CMC data can be done without loss of generality (see Remark 3.7).

Lemma 3.5

(Local well-posedness for the ESFV system in harmonic gauge). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l\in \mathbb {N},\,l\ge 3$$\end{document}l∈N,l≥3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathbb {R},\,\mu \ge 3$$\end{document}μ∈R,μ≥3. Further, let m=0,1m=0,1 denote the Vlasov mass, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\pi },\mathring{\psi },\mathring{f})$$\end{document}(g˚,k˚,π˚,ψ˚,f˚) be initial data to the ESFV system (1.1) as in Sect. 2.3 with 3.6a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathring{g}\Vert _{H^{l+1}_\gamma (M)}+\Vert \mathring{k}\Vert _{H^{l}_\gamma (M)}+\Vert \mathring{\pi }\Vert _{H^l_\gamma (M)}+\Vert \mathring{\psi }\Vert _{H^l_\gamma (M)}<\infty \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖g˚‖Hγl+1(M)+‖k˚‖Hγl(M)+‖π˚‖Hγl(M)+‖ψ˚‖Hγl(M)<∞as well as3.6b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathring{f}\in H^{l}_{\mu ,\underline{\gamma }_0,m}(T^*M_{t_0}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f˚∈Hμ,γ_0,ml(T∗Mt0), Then, a local solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{M},\overline{g},\nabla \phi ,\partial _0\phi ,f)$$\end{document}(M¯,g¯,∇ϕ,∂0ϕ,f) in harmonic gauge with Vlasov-mass m exists in the following sense: For some h>0h>0 and writing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J=(t_0-h,t_0+h)$$\end{document}J=(t0-h,t0+h), a spacetime manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{M}$$\end{document}M¯ can be foliated by spacelike Cauchy hypersurfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M_t)_{t\in J}$$\end{document}(Mt)t∈J, each of which is endowed with a collection of coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t\equiv x_U^0,x_U^1,x_U^2,x_U^3)$$\end{document}(t≡xU0,xU1,xU2,xU3), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} Proof

(Proof-Outline.) As argued, for example, in [Rin13, Section 22.2.3], the Einstein equations and the wave equation for the scalar field can be modified in harmonic gauge such that one can equivalently solve a quasilinear system in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{g}_{\mu \nu }$$\end{document}g¯μν and ϕ ϕ, coupled with the transport equation for ff. For such systems, [Rin13, Proposition 19.76 and Lemma 19.83] lead to the stated result for m=1m=1, and respectively [Sve12, Paper B, Proposition 8.48 and Lemma 9.1] for m=0m=0, since one easily checks that all operators and nonlinearities remain admissible when no scalar field potential is present (see [Rin13, Section 19.1], respectively [Sve12, Paper B, Section 8.1]). We also note that, while the results in [Rin13, Sve12] are proven while viewing the Vlasov distribution as a function on the mass shell, rather than the co-mass shell, we can induce initial data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{f}^\sharp :TM\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f˚♯:TM→R on the mass shell by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{f}^\sharp (x,q)=\mathring{f}\left( x,p(q,\mathring{g})\right) ,\quad p_i(q,\mathring{g})=\mathring{g}_{ij}\,q^j\,,$$\end{document}f˚♯(x,q)=f˚x,p(q,g˚),pi(q,g˚)=g˚ijqj,which, due to the regularity requirements for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} We note that the non-negativity of the Vlasov distribution function is ensured by the Vlasov equation, and can be argued as in Lemma 4.5. Furthermore, uniqueness follows from uniqueness of the maximal globally hyperbolic development (see Sect. 2.3).

To prove that we can take initial data to be CMC without loss of generality, we need the following result:

Lemma 3.6

(Cauchy stability of near-FLRW solutions to the ESFV system). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l\in \mathbb {N},\ l\ge 3$$\end{document}l∈N,l≥3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \in \mathbb {R},\ \mu \ge 3$$\end{document}μ∈R,μ≥3, and let δ >0δ>0 be sufficiently small. Further, consider initial data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M,\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\pi },\mathring{\psi },\mathring{f})$$\end{document}(M,g˚,k˚,π˚,ψ˚,f˚) to the ESFV system with Vlasov mass m=0,1m=0,1 (again as in Sect. 2.3) such that one has 3.9a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathring{g}-a(t_0)^2\gamma \Vert _{H^{l+1}_\gamma (M)}+\Vert \mathring{k}+a(t_0)\dot{a}(t_0)\gamma \Vert _{H^{l}_\gamma (M)}+\Vert \mathring{\pi }\Vert _{H^l_\gamma (M)}+\Vert \mathring{\psi }-Ca(t_0)^{-3}\Vert _{H^l_\gamma (M)}<\delta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖g˚-a(t0)2γ‖Hγl+1(M)+‖k˚+a(t0)a˙(t0)γ‖Hγl(M)+‖π˚‖Hγl(M)+‖ψ˚-Ca(t0)-3‖Hγl(M)<δand3.9b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert (\mathring{f}-f_{FLRW})(t_0,\cdot ,\cdot )\Vert _{H^{l}_{\mu ,\underline{\gamma }_0,m}(T^*M)}<\delta \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖(f˚-fFLRW)(t0,·,·)‖Hμ,γ_0,ml(T∗M)<δ. hold. In the massless Vlasov case, we additionally assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{f}$$\end{document}f˚ vanishes in an open neighbourhood of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{(x,p)\in T^*M \,\vert \, p=0\}$$\end{document}{(x,p)∈T∗M|p=0}. Then the solution in the sense of Lemma 3.5 satisfies the following bound for some K>0K>0 that is independent of δ >0δ>0 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [t_1,t_0], t_1>\mathfrak {t}$$\end{document}t∈[t1,t0],t1>t:3.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert g-a^2\gamma \Vert _{H_\gamma ^{l+1}(M_t)}+\Vert k-\frac{\tau }{3} a^2\gamma \Vert _{H_\gamma ^l(M_t)}+\Vert \nabla \phi \Vert _{H_\gamma ^l(M_t)}&\nonumber \\+\Vert \partial _0\phi -Ca^{-3}\Vert _{H_\gamma ^l(M_t)} +\Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{H^l_{\mu ,\underline{\gamma }_0,m}(T^*M_t)}&\,\le K\delta \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖g-a2γ‖Hγl+1(Mt)+‖k-τ3a2γ‖Hγl(Mt)+‖∇ϕ‖Hγl(Mt)+‖∂0ϕ-Ca-3‖Hγl(Mt)+‖f-fFLRW‖Hμ,γ_0,ml(T∗Mt)≤Kδ.

Proof

(Proof-Outline.) For the massive case, this can be proven as in [Rin13, Corollary 24.10], which in turn follows along similar lines as [Rin09a, Theorem 15.10]. Essentially, the argument therein reduces to using the respective result for the metric components and matter variables in harmonic gauge from [Rin13, Corollary 20.7] and patching these local results to a spatially global result. The aforementioned statements are, at first glance, only applicable for smooth initial data, but this clearly extends to non-smooth data by a standard approximation argument.

This mostly applies analogously to m=0m=0—the only issue that arises is that (3.8b) is regularity dependent, thus solutions at high regularity might only be extendible in lower regularity. However, if the distribution function vanishes on an open neighbourhood of the zero section throughout the evolution, any choice of l\ge 3l≥3 in (3.8b) is equivalent to any other (see the proof of [Sve12, Paper B, Lemma 9.2]). That this is the case can be shown as in [Sve12, Paper B, Lemma 8.25], analogous to the proof of (4.7). □ □

Remark 3.7

(Existence of a CMC hypersurface) Before moving on to our main well-posedness result in CMC gauge with zero shift, we illustrate why the above results imply that, as in [RS18b, Spe18, FU25], it is is not a true restriction to take initial data to be CMC :

If the initial data is close to FLRW data as in (3.4), but not CMC, Lemma 3.5 yields a local-in-time solution by evolving in harmonic gauge, and by Lemma 3.6, we can restrict ourselves to a time interval such that we remain close to the FLRW data throughout the local time evolution. An implicit function theorem argument as in [FK20, Section 2.5] now implies that, within this local solution, we can find a Cauchy hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma ^\prime $$\end{document}Σ′ close to the initial hypersurface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{t_0}$$\end{document}Mt0 that is CMC. The adaptation of the argument in [FK20, Section 2.5] can be done as sketched in [FU25, Remark 8.1]: The only noteworthy change compared to the latter is in computing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\overline{g}_{FLRW},t_0)$$\end{document}(g¯FLRW,t0) remains a regular point of the mean curvature map H under the addition of Vlasov matter and regardless of sectional curvature. One computes using (2.3a):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & a(t_0)^2dH_{(\overline{g}_{FLRW},t_0)}(0,w)\\ & \qquad =\left[ \frac{1}{3}\Delta _\gamma +\kappa -4\pi C^2a(t_0)^{-4}-4\pi a(t_0)^{-2}\left( \rho _{FLRW}^{Vl}+\mathfrak {p}_{FLRW}^{Vl}\right) \right] w\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a(t0)2dH(g¯FLRW,t0)(0,w)=13Δγ+κ-4πC2a(t0)-4-4πa(t0)-2ρFLRWVl+pFLRWVlw.Since we assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{FLRW}\ge 0$$\end{document}fFLRW≥0 and C>0C>0, and since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _\gamma $$\end{document}Δγ has no positive eigenvalues, this clearly is a regular point for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \le 0$$\end{document}κ≤0. For κ >0κ>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa <4\pi C^2a(t_0)^{-4}$$\end{document}κ<4πC2a(t0)-4 can be ensured for small enough \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0>0$$\end{document}t0>0, which Lemma 3.6 allows us to take without loss of generality.

Having obtained a CMC hypersurface, we need to ensure that Lemma 3.6 is sufficient to ensure Assumption 3.4 for suitably small δ >0δ>0. This follows from Sobolev embedding for all terms except those associated with Vlasov matter: The Vlasov equation ensures that f is still nonnegative (as in Lemma 4.5), and that it has compact momentum support as well as, in the massless case, support bounded away from the zero section (as in Lemma 4.6). In particular, for δ <1δ<1, any momentum weights can be dropped or added up to a constant depending on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}fFLRW, and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {f}-{f}_{FLRW}\Vert _{H^{18}_{1,\underline{\gamma }_0}(T^*M_t)}\lesssim \delta $$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖H1,γ_018(T∗Mt)≲δ also holds. Using a co-mass shell analogue of [Rin13, Lemma 15.29] or estimates similar to the energy bounds in Lemma 4.14, this ensures the equivalent bound on all Vlasov matter terms in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C after potentially updating constants. Finally, (3.5) can be ensured since [Rin13, Lemma 15.29] applied analogously to the co-mass shell and the standard Sobolev embedding on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3 imply\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert {f}-{f}_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^{11}_{1,\underline{\gamma }_0}(T^*M_t)}\lesssim \sup _{x\in M}\Vert {f}-{f}_{FLRW}\Vert _{L^\infty _xH^{13}_{1,\underline{\gamma }_0\vert _{vert}}(T^*_xM_t)}\lesssim \Vert {f}-{f}_{FLRW}\Vert _{H^{13}_{1,\underline{\gamma }_0}(T^*M_t)}\,.$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C1,γ_011(T∗Mt)≲supx∈M‖f-fFLRW‖Lx∞H1,γ_0|vert13(Tx∗Mt)≲‖f-fFLRW‖H1,γ_013(T∗Mt).Again, we note that, on the momentum support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}-{f}_{FLRW}$$\end{document}f-fFLRW on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[t_1,t_0]\times M$$\end{document}[t1,t0]×M, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle v\rangle _\gamma \simeq 1$$\end{document}⟨v⟩γ≃1 holds.

Remark 3.8

(Smallness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0$$\end{document}t0) To establish a strong total energy bound in Proposition 7.6, we will need to assume that a(t) is sufficiently small for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], which simply means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0$$\end{document}t0 needs to be sufficiently small since a is strictly increasing on (0, T/2), see Lemma 2.2. Lemma 3.6 ensures that, if we choose the initial perturbation to be small enough, we can use Cauchy stability to ensure that the solution remains sufficiently close to the FLRW solution in any fixed time interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[t_1,t_0]$$\end{document}[t1,t0]. We can then run the argument in Remark 3.7 to find a nearby CMC hypersurface, and can thus overall assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0>0$$\end{document}t0>0 to be sufficiently small without loss of generality.

Having now prepared initial data to be CMC as well as sufficiently close to the Big Bang if needed, we can state our main local well-posedness result:

Lemma 3.9

(Local well-posedness in CMC gauge with zero shift). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(M,\mathring{g},\mathring{k},\mathring{\pi },\mathring{\psi },\mathring{f})$$\end{document}(M,g˚,k˚,π˚,ψ˚,f˚) be CMC initial data as in Lemma 3.5 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \ge 3$$\end{document}μ≥3. Proof

(Proof-Outline.) In short, this can be proven along the same lines as in the proof of [RS18b, Theorem 14.1] for the stiff fluid, with the analysis of Vlasov matter following from [Rin13, Sve12] as above. The continuation criterion (3.11a) then extends from the scalar field case, (3.11b) is the direct analogue of (3.11a) for the Vlasov matter quantities that occur in the elliptic-hyperbolic system for the metric variables, and (3.11c) is needed to ensure boundedness of coefficients arising from Vlasov energy estimates as in the proof in harmonic gauge.

To be more precise, the proof of [RS18b, Theorem 14.1] relies on showing that a modified system of equations leads to an elliptic-hyperbolic system in which the second fundamental form satisfies a wave equation, where its evolution equation is considered as an additional constraint quantity. The only properties of stiff fluid matter that are used in the proof are that the stiff fluid equation implies that the associated energy-momentum tensor being divergence-free, that it satisfies the strong energy condition and that it generates standard energy estimates. The former two are also satisfied for our matter model (where we again note that one has f\ge 0f≥0, and thus the strong energy condition is ensured). Since scalar field matter constitutes a subcase of stiff fluid matter, the energy estimates for stiff fluids apply to the scalar field as well. Thus, energy estimates for the geometry and scalar field can be straightforwardly extended from the stiff fluid setting.

The necessary energy estimates for the Vlasov distribution function can be established as in [Rin13, Sve12], where they are proven for a wide class of relativistic transport operators of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L\xi =\partial _t\xi +\frac{{z^i}}{q^0}\partial _{x^i}\xi +\frac{G^i}{q^0}\partial _{q^i}\xi \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lξ=∂tξ+ziq0∂xiξ+Giq0∂qiξ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z^i\equiv z^i[u]$$\end{document}zi≡zi[u] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^i\equiv G^i[u]$$\end{document}Gi≡Gi[u] are sufficiently regular coefficient functions depending on the quantities u that solve the coupled system of wave equations, which correspond to the spacetime metric components. Again, note that these statements are proven for the mass shell, so we need to consider the Vlasov equation on the mass shell, i.e., apply this to the equation3.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Lf^\sharp =\partial _t\xi +\frac{q^i}{q^0}\,n\,\partial _{x^i}f^\sharp +\left( -\frac{q^iq^l}{q^0}\,n\,\Gamma [g]^j_{il}-q^0\,g^{ij}\,\nabla _in+2n\,k^{j}_{\ i}\,q^i\right) \partial _{q^j}\xi =0\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lf♯=∂tξ+qiq0n∂xif♯+-qiqlq0nΓ[g]ilj-q0gij∇in+2nkijqi∂qjξ=0.Nevertheless, we can establish analogous estimates for these components using the elliptic-hyperbolic system from [RS18b, Theorem 14.1], and the transport operator for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^\sharp $$\end{document}f♯ is actually simpler than the one that needs to be considered in harmonic gauge, energy estimates for the Vlasov distribution also extend. Finally, the components of the Vlasov energy momentum tensor can be bounded up to constant by the energies for the distribution function as in Lemma 4.14.

Thus, we can prove local well-posedness of the modified elliptic-hyperbolic system metric and scalar field coupled to the Vlasov equation by a constructing a similar convergent sequence as in [Rin13, Proposition 19.76] and [Sve12, Paper B, Proposition 8.48]. That this then also solves the Einstein equations—i.e., that the constraints are propagated—can again be proven as in the stiff fluid setting since we only use properties of the energy momentum tensor shared by both matter models.

The blow-up criteria follow by combining what arises from the elliptic-hyperbolic system (extending from [RS18b, Theorem 14.1]) with those arising from the transport equation from Lemma 3.5. For the latter, (3.8a) is covered by the requirements (3.11a) from the elliptic-hyperbolic system, and thus only (3.8b) needs to be added. □ □

Remark 3.10

(On regularity of norms and energies) In addition to the initial data assumptions (see Assumption 3.4), we can assume, without loss of generality by a standard approximation argument, that the initial data is sufficiently regular. More precisely, using Lemma 3.9, we will assume tacitly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}l∈N to be sufficiently large such that all norms and energies are continuously differentiable in time. We note that, in the massless case, the blow-up criterion (3.8b) is dependent on how regular we choose our initial data to be a priori. However, due to control on the support of f (see Lemma 4.6), it will turn out that this additional weight can be ignored when checking this criterion (see Proof of Theorem 8.1).

Bootstrap assumptions

By Lemma 3.9, initial data as in Assumption 3.4 generates a local-in-time solution toward the past such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}(t)$$\end{document}C(t) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^{11}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C1,G_011(T∗Mt) are continuous in t. Thus, the following bootstrap assumption can be satisfied:

Assumption 3.11

(Bootstrap assumptions). We assume that, for the bootstrap time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_{Boot}\in [0,t_0)$$\end{document}tBoot∈[0,t0), the following holds for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0]: 3.13a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}(t)\le K_0\varepsilon a^{-c_0\sigma }\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(t)≤K0εa-c0σ.Additionally, we assume3.13b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \underline{\nabla }_{hor}(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert _{C^{10}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}\le K_0{\varepsilon }^\frac{1}{4}a^{-c_0\sigma }\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∇_hor(f-fFLRW)‖C1,G_010(T∗Mt)≤K0ε14a-c0σ.

Our goal is to show that the bootstrap assumptions imply\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}\le K_1\varepsilon ^\frac{7}{4} a^{-c_1\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}},\quad \Vert \underline{\nabla }_{hor}(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert _{C^{10}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}\le K_1\sqrt{\varepsilon } a^{-c_1\sqrt{\varepsilon }} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C≤K1ε74a-c1ε18,‖∇_hor(f-fFLRW)‖C1,G_010(T∗Mt)≤K1εa-c1εfor suitable constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_1,K_1>0$$\end{document}c1,K1>0 and any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], which is a strict improvement if \varepsilon >0ε>0 is chosen sufficiently small. As with implicit constants in the notation “≲ ≲”, see Sect. 2.1, i.e., such updates may depend on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0>0$$\end{document}t0>0 as well as quantities derived from the FLRW reference spacetime and abstract constants in the proof, including \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_0$$\end{document}K0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0$$\end{document}c0.

Remark 3.12

(Lapse bounds) We note that, in particular, (3.13a) implies3.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert N\Vert _{C^{14}_G(M_t)}\lesssim \varepsilon a(t)^{2-c\sigma }\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖N‖CG14(Mt)≲εa(t)2-cσ,and we will in fact only be able to improve the convergence rate to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon a^{2-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}$$\end{document}εa2-cε18 by Theorem 8.1. Thus, the lapse converges less strongly than in the pure scalar field case, and the bootstrap assumption is also weaker (see [FU25, (3.17h)]). This is due to Vlasov matter being the asymptotically strongest term in (2.21g). However, it does not lead to substantial changes in the argument since the fact that the lapse converges at a rate stronger than, for example, a(t) for sufficiently small σ >0σ>0 is enough to make otherwise borderline terms containing low orders of N converge.

A Priori Estimates

Strong low order bounds on spacetime metric and scalar field variables

In this section, we collect the necessary improved low order estimates for all variables that are not the Vlasov distribution function or associated matter quantities. Since Vlasov matter is asympotically negligible in the evolution of metric variables and the shear as well as in the Hamiltonian and Bel-Robinson constraint equations, and does not occur in the wave equation at all, all of these bounds follow almost directly from [FU25, Sections 4.1 and 4.2], and we only sketch how one controls the occuring Vlasov terms with the bootstrap assumption.

Lemma 4.1

(Strong \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^0_G$$\end{document}CG0 bounds). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], the following estimates hold: 4.1a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Psi \Vert _{C^0_G(M_t)}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ψ‖CG0(Mt)≲ε,4.1b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Sigma \Vert _{C^0_G(M_t)}+\Vert \Sigma ^\sharp \Vert _{C^0_\gamma (M_t)}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Σ‖CG0(Mt)+‖Σ♯‖Cγ0(Mt)≲ε,4.1c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {E}\Vert _{C^0_G(M_t)}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖E‖CG0(Mt)≲ε.

Proof

(4.1a) is proven identically to the scalar field case (see [FU25, (4.4a)]), replacing the lapse bootstrap assumption therein with (3.14).

Regarding (4.1b), one has4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\partial _t|\Sigma |_G^2 = 2{\left( \partial _t\Sigma ^\sharp \right) ^i}_j{\left( \Sigma ^\sharp \right) ^j}_i\lesssim \left\lvert (\partial _t\Sigma ^\sharp )^\parallel \right\rvert _G|\Sigma |_G\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-∂t|Σ|G2=2∂tΣ♯ijΣ♯ji≲(∂tΣ♯)‖G|Σ|G,where we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\partial _t\Sigma ^\sharp )^\parallel $$\end{document}(∂tΣ♯)‖ is the tracefree, symmetric part of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t\Sigma ^\sharp $$\end{document}∂tΣ♯. Estimating all non-Vlasov terms as in the proof of [FU25, (4.2b)] using (3.13a), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\lvert \partial _t\Sigma ^\sharp \right\rvert _G\lesssim \varepsilon a^{-1-c\sigma }(1+|\Sigma |_G)+a^{-1}\left\lvert S^{Vl,\parallel }\right\rvert _G\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tΣ♯G≲εa-1-cσ(1+|Σ|G)+a-1SVl,‖G.That this bound is weaker than its analogue in the pure scalar field case is, again, down to (3.14). The Vlasov term can also be bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lesssim \varepsilon a^{-1-c\sigma }$$\end{document}≲εa-1-cσ using the bootstrap assumption (3.13a). Integrating (4.2) and inserting this bound along with (3.4) and (2.6) then implies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\Sigma |_G^2(t)\lesssim |\Sigma |_G^2(t_0)+\int _t^{t_0}\varepsilon a(s)^{-1-c\sigma }\,ds\lesssim \varepsilon . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Σ|G2(t)≲|Σ|G2(t0)+∫t0εa(s)-1-cσds≲ε.The bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\Sigma ^\sharp |_{C^0_\gamma }$$\end{document}|Σ♯|Cγ0 is proven identically since the bootstrap assumption on G-γ G-γ ensures that all quantities in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C satisfy the bootstrap assumptions with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_G$$\end{document}CG replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\gamma $$\end{document}Cγ, up to updating constants.

(4.1c) then follows directly from (2.21j): Due to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle G,\mathcal {E}\rangle _G=0$$\end{document}⟨G,E⟩G=0, the second line in (2.21j) can be ignored, and using (4.1b) for all shear terms and (3.13a) for all other terms, including Vlasov matter, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \mathcal {E},\mathcal {E}\rangle _G\lesssim \varepsilon |\mathcal {E}|_G$$\end{document}⟨E,E⟩G≲ε|E|G and thus the statement. □ □

(4.3a), (4.3c)–(4.3e) and (4.3g) are also proven almost entirely identically to their analogues in [FU25, Lemma 4.3], the only differences arising again from the weaker bootstrap bound (3.14) on the lapse that is, as with the shear, sufficient to prove that terms containing N are asymptotically negligible. Lemma 4.3

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _G=\sqrt{\det G}$$\end{document}μG=detG and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], one has4.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mu _G-\mu _\gamma \Vert _{C^0(M_t)}\lesssim \varepsilon \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖μG-μγ‖C0(Mt)≲ε.

Proof

This follows immediately from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t\mu _G=-N\tau \mu _G$$\end{document}∂tμG=-NτμG, (3.14), the initial data assumption and the Gronwall lemma. □ □

Lemma 4.5

(Non-negativity of the Vlasov distribution function). For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(t,\cdot ,\cdot )$$\end{document}f(t,·,·) is nonnegative, as well as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{Vl}(t,\cdot )$$\end{document}ρVl(t,·) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}(t,\cdot )$$\end{document}pVl(t,·).

Proof

Recall that s↦ f(s,X(s),V(s))s↦f(s,X(s),V(s)) remains constant for characteristics (X, V) satisfying (4.5) and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathring{f}$$\end{document}f˚ is nonnegative. Thus, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,v)\in T^*M_t$$\end{document}(x,v)∈T∗Mt, there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_{init},v_{init})\in T^*M$$\end{document}(xinit,vinit)∈T∗M such that one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\le \mathring{f}(x_{init},v_{init})=f(t_0,x_{init},v_{init})=f(t,x,v)\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0≤f˚(xinit,vinit)=f(t0,xinit,vinit)=f(t,x,v).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^0$$\end{document}v0 is also non-negative, so are then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{Vl}(t,\cdot )$$\end{document}ρVl(t,·) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}(t,\cdot )$$\end{document}pVl(t,·). □ □

Lemma 4.6

(Bounds for the momentum support). There exists a constant K>0K>0 that is independent of \varepsilon ε such that the following bounds hold: 4.6a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {P}(t)\le \mathcal {P}^0(t)\le&\,Ka(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(t)≤P0(t)≤Ka(t)-cε,4.6b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {P}_\gamma (t)\le \mathcal {P}_\gamma ^0(t)\le&\,K\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pγ(t)≤Pγ0(t)≤K. Lemma 4.8

(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^0_{1,\cdot }$$\end{document}C1,·0-estimates for the Vlasov distribution function). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], one has: 4.14a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^0_{1,\underline{\gamma }_0}(T^*M_t)}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C1,γ_00(T∗Mt)≲ε,4.14b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^0_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C1,G_00(T∗Mt)≲εa(t)-cε.

Proof

We start out by rewriting the rescaled Vlasov equation (2.20a) as an inhomogeneous transport equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} Lemma 4.9

(Strong \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{l}_{1,\underline{G}_0}$$\end{document}C1,G_0l-norm estimates for the Vlasov distribution function). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0]. Then, the following holds:4.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^{11}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}\lesssim \sqrt{\varepsilon }a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C1,G_011(T∗Mt)≲εa(t)-cε.

Note that, for \varepsilon >0ε>0 suitably small, (4.16) is a bootstrap improvement for (3.13b).

Proof

The following proof will require us to choose a spatial coordinate frame to express horizontal frame derivatives, so we must first establish suitable coordinate systems to cover the entire evolution: Since M is compact, there exists some sufficiently small r>0r>0 such that an open ball \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_r(x)$$\end{document}Br(x) of radius r>0r>0 around any x∈ Mx∈M is a coordinate neighbourhood, and there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_1,\dots ,x_m\in M$$\end{document}x1,⋯,xm∈M such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{B_{\frac{r}{2}}(x_i)\}_{i=1,\dots ,m}$$\end{document}{Br2(xi)}i=1,⋯,m covers M. Furthermore, for (4.5a), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\frac{d}{dt}X|_G\lesssim a^{-1}$$\end{document}|ddtX|G≲a-1 holds, which is integrable on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0,t_0]$$\end{document}[0,t0] (see (2.6)), since |N+1|≲ 1|N+1|≲1 holds by the bootstrap assumption (3.14). (4.5a) will describe the spatial characteristics for all systems considered in this proof. In particular, there exists a finite partition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}(tBoot,t0] by intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_{j},t_{j-1}]$$\end{document}(tj,tj-1] such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{t_{j+1}}^{t_j}a(s)^{-1}\,ds<\frac{r}{2}$$\end{document}∫tj+1ja(s)-1ds<r2 holds. Thus, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in B_{\frac{r}{2}}(x_i)$$\end{document}y∈Br2(xi), the characteristic X emanating from y at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_j$$\end{document}tj is contained in the coordinate neighbourhood \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_r(x_i)$$\end{document}Br(xi) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_{j},t_{j-1}]$$\end{document}(tj,tj-1]. Thus, we can prove (4.16) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_1,t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(t1,t0] using local coordinates by having found a choice of coordinates that remains valid throughout the evolution, and since we can cover M in a finite amount of such coordinate neighbourhoods. Finally, the bounds then extend to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1$$\end{document}t1 by continuity, and thus we can prove the statement iteratively on the entire bootstrap interval by proving the bound on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_{j},t_{j-1}]$$\end{document}(tj,tj-1] from data emanating from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{t_{j-1}}$$\end{document}Mtj-1.

With (4.3c), this also implies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert _{\dot{C}^1_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}\lesssim&\,\sqrt{\varepsilon }a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f-fFLRW‖C˙1,G_01(T∗Mt)≲εa(t)-cε.We now sketch how to extend this procedure to higher orders inductively, since the line of argumentation is almost identical: Assume the improved bound has been proven up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{{L}-1}_{1,\underline{G}_0}$$\end{document}C1,G_0L-1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${L}\le 11$$\end{document}L≤11.
