Additionally, the bounds on the Vlasov distribution function admit bounds on its energy-momentum tensor:

Corollary 4.10

(Strong low order bounds on Vlasov matter quantities). The following bounds hold:4.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \rho ^{Vl}-\rho ^{Vl}_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^{11}_G(M_t)}+\Vert \mathfrak {p}^{Vl}-\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}_{FLRW}\Vert _{C^{11}_G(M_t)}+\Vert \jmath ^{Vl}\Vert _{C^{11}_G(M_t)}+\Vert S^{Vl,\parallel }\Vert _{C^{11}_G(M_t)}\nonumber \\&\quad \lesssim \sqrt{\varepsilon }a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ρVl-ρFLRWVl‖CG11(Mt)+‖pVl-pFLRWVl‖CG11(Mt)+‖ȷVl‖CG11(Mt)+‖SVl,‖‖CG11(Mt)≲εa(t)-cε.

uniform estimates (4.14b) and (4.3c).

Regarding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^{Vl,\parallel }$$\end{document}SVl,‖, we compute\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {(S^{Vl,\parallel })^i}_j=&\,\int _{T^*_\cdot M}\frac{(G^{-1})^{il}v_lv_j-\frac{1}{3}|v|_G^2\mathbb {I}^i_j}{v^0}(f-f_{FLRW})(\cdot ,\cdot ,v)\mu _G^{-1}\,dv\\&\,+\int _{T^*_\cdot M}\left[ \left( \frac{(G^{-1})^{il}}{v^0}-\frac{(\gamma ^{-1})^{il}}{v^0_\gamma }\right) v_lv_j-\frac{1}{3}\left( \frac{|v|_G^2}{v^0}-\frac{|v|_\gamma ^2}{v^0_\gamma }\right) \mathbb {I}^i_j\right] \,\mathcal {F}(|v|_\gamma ^2)\mu _G^{-1}\,dv\\&\,+\frac{\mu _\gamma }{\mu _G}\left( {(S^{Vl}_{FLRW})^i}_j-\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}_{FLRW}\mathbb {I}^i_j\right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(SVl,‖)ij=∫T·∗M(G-1)ilvlvj-13|v|G2Ijiv0(f-fFLRW)(·,·,v)μG-1dv+∫T·∗M(G-1)ilv0-(γ-1)ilvγ0vlvj-13|v|G2v0-|v|γ2vγ0IjiF(|v|γ2)μG-1dv+μγμG(SFLRWVl)ij-pFLRWVlIji.Note that the final line vanishes since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}fFLRW is isotropic. Regarding the second line, note one has, with (4.3c),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\lvert \frac{1}{v^0}-\frac{1}{v^0_\gamma }\right\rvert =\frac{\left\lvert (v^0)^2-(v^0_\gamma )^2\right\rvert }{v^0\,v^0_\gamma \,\left( v^0+v^0_\gamma \right) }\le \frac{|G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}|_\gamma }{v^0}\lesssim a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,\frac{|G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}|_G}{v^0}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1v0-1vγ0=(v0)2-(vγ0)2v0vγ0v0+vγ0≤|G-1-γ-1|γv0≲a-cε|G-1-γ-1|Gv0.Consequently, the pointwise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\cdot |_G$$\end{document}|·|G-norm of the second line is bounded by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lesssim a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,|G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}|_G\,\int _{T^*_\cdot M}v_\gamma ^0\,\mathcal {F}(|v|_\gamma ^2)\,\mu _\gamma ^{-1}\,dv\lesssim \sqrt{\varepsilon } a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}≲a-cε|G-1-γ-1|G∫T·∗Mvγ0F(|v|γ2)μγ-1dv≲εa-cεusing (4.3c). From here, the argument follows as for the density. Bounds for the pressure and flux are proven similarly at order zero.

At order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\in \{1,\dots ,11\}$$\end{document}L∈{1,⋯,11}, note that one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla ^L\rho ^{Vl}_{FLRW}=\nabla ^L\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}_{FLRW}=0$$\end{document}∇LρFLRWVl=∇LpFLRWVl=0 as well as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \underline{\nabla }^L_{hor}f\Vert _{C^{0}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M)}\lesssim \sqrt{\varepsilon }a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∇_horLf‖C1,G_00(T∗M)≲εa-cεusing (4.16) and (4.11c). The higher order bounds now follow in a similar, but simpler fashion to the arguments at order zero using (4.6a). □ □

Finally, we collect the following lemma that will be needed to bound the evolution of weights in our energy approach:

Lemma 4.11

(Evolution bound on momentum variables).4.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\frac{\partial _tv^0}{v^0}\lesssim \varepsilon a^{-3}\,,\quad -\frac{\partial _t\langle v\rangle _G}{\langle v\rangle _G}\lesssim \varepsilon a^{-3}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-∂tv0v0≲εa-3,-∂t⟨v⟩G⟨v⟩G≲εa-3.

Proof

One computes the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _tv^0=\frac{1}{2v^0}\left[ 2m^2a\dot{a}+(\partial _tG^{-1})^{ij}v_iv_j\right] =(N+1)a^{-3}\Sigma ^{\sharp ij}\frac{v_iv_j}{v^0}-N\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\frac{|v|_G^2}{v^0}+\frac{\dot{a}}{a}\cdot \frac{m^2a^2}{v^0}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv0=12v02m2aa˙+(∂tG-1)ijvivj=(N+1)a-3Σ♯ijvivjv0-Na˙a|v|G2v0+a˙a·m2a2v0.The statement then follows from (4.1b) and (3.13a), where the final summand has favourable sign and is thus dropped. The estimate for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle v\rangle _G$$\end{document}⟨v⟩G is obtained identically. □ □

Semi-norm equivalences and commutators for the Vlasov distribution

In this section, we collect estimates on covariant commutator errors for functions on the mass shell, that in particular will imply that the Vlasov energies (3.3f)–(3.3g) are coercive up to error terms dependent on lower order Vlasov energies as well as metric norms and energies. This will be sufficient to deduce control of the Vlasov matter quantities in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C from improved energy bounds in Sect. 7.

Lemma 4.12

(Commutator formulas for the Sasaki metric). Let ξ ξ be a sufficiently regular function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\displaystyle \cup _{t\in (t_{Boot},t_0], x\in M}P_{(t,x)}$$\end{document}∪t∈(tBoot,t0],x∈MP(t,x) and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1,M_2\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}M1,M2∈N. by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} By Lemma (4.6), (4.25) applies for ξ =fξ=f.

Proof

These formulas follow from straightforward computations using the commutator formulas in Lemma 2.8 and the connection coefficient expressions from (2.17). For example, considering (4.25a), the second term in the first line is a direct consequence of (2.26a), recalling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\underline{\nabla }_{i},\underline{\nabla }_{j}]\xi =\underline{\nabla }_{[\textbf{A}_i,\textbf{A}_j]}\xi $$\end{document}[∇_i,∇_j]ξ=∇_[Ai,Aj]ξ, and the second line arises from further horizontal derivatives hitting this term. Meanwhile, the first term in the first line arises from commuting horizontal and vertical covariant derivatives and, in particular, the connection coefficients in (2.17b).

Regarding (4.24b), one writes out the expression in the local frame \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\textbf{A}_i,\textbf{B}^j\}$$\end{document}{Ai,Bj} and observes that, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t\underline{\Gamma }$$\end{document}∂tΓ_ extends to a tensor field, this recombines to the claimed schematic expression globally. The leading order terms in the first line are, respectively, the highest order spatial derivative term arising from terms of the type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^{M_2-2}\left( \partial _t{\underline{\Gamma }^{\underline{k+3}}}_{\underline{i}\,\underline{j}}\right) $$\end{document}∇_horM2-2∂tΓ_k+3_i_j_ and the highest horizontal order term arising from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t{\underline{\Gamma }^{\underline{k+3}}}_{\underline{i+3}\,\underline{j}}$$\end{document}∂tΓ_k+3_i+3_j_ as well as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\partial _t,\underline{\nabla }_j]\xi $$\end{document}[∂t,∇_j]ξ.

For both error bounds, we use the assumption on the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi (t,\cdot ,\cdot )$$\end{document}ξ(t,·,·)—e.g., for f, the bounds (4.6a) on the momentum support—and estimate lower order curvature and metric errors with (4.3e) and (4.3c) to obtain (4.25a). For (4.24b) specifically, also recall (2.21e) and (2.21d) and use (4.3b) as well as the bootstrap assumption (3.13a) to bound lower order terms in the rescaled shear and lapse respectively. □ □

Lemma 4.13

(Rearrangement of derivatives in intermediate Vlasov energies). Let 0<K<Lłe 190<K<L≤19 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{L,K}$$\end{document}DL,K denote a combination of L-KL-K vertical and K horizontal covariant derivatives with respect to the Sasaki metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{G}$$\end{document}G_. Then, the following holds:4.26a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left\lvert \Vert \mathcal {D}_{L,K}(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert ^2_{L^2_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}-\Vert \underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^K(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert ^2_{L^2_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}\right\rvert \nonumber \\&\quad \lesssim \,a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\left( \mathfrak {E}^{(\le L-1)}_{1,\le K}(f,t)+\varepsilon {\mathfrak {E}^{(\le K-1)}(\text { {Ric}},t)}+\Vert \Gamma -\hat{\Gamma }\Vert _{H^{K-1}_G(M_t)}^2+\Vert G^{\pm 1}-\gamma ^{\pm 1}\Vert _{H^{K-1}_G(M_t)}^2\right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖DL,K(f-fFLRW)‖L1,G_02(T∗Mt)2-‖∇_vertL-K∇_horK(f-fFLRW)‖L1,G_02(T∗Mt)2≲a(t)-cεE1,≤K(≤L-1)(f,t)+εE(≤K-1)(Ric,t)+‖Γ-Γ^‖HGK-1(Mt)2+‖G±1-γ±1‖HGK-1(Mt)2.Similarly, one has for 1łe K<L1≤K<L,4.26b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\lvert \Vert \mathcal {D}_{L,K}f\Vert _{L^2_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M_t)}^2-\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,t)\right\rvert \lesssim&\,a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\mathfrak {E}^{(\le L-1)}_{1,\le K}(f,t)+\varepsilon a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\mathfrak {E}^{(\le K-1)}(\text { {Ric}},t)\nonumber \\&\,+a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\left( \Vert \Gamma -\hat{\Gamma }\Vert _{L^2_G(M_t)}^2+\Vert G^{\pm 1}-\gamma ^{\pm 1}\Vert _{H^1_G(M_t)}^2\right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖DL,Kf‖L1,G_02(T∗Mt)2-E1,K(L)(f,t)≲a(t)-cεE1,≤K(≤L-1)(f,t)+εa(t)-cεE(≤K-1)(Ric,t)+a(t)-cε‖Γ-Γ^‖LG2(Mt)2+‖G±1-γ±1‖HG1(Mt)2.If one additionally takes Kłe 10K≤10 in any of the above bounds, all terms but the Vlasov energy quantities can be dropped.

Proof

Reviewing the commutator formulas from Lemma 2.8, one schematically has for K>0K>0:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}_{L,K}\xi -\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^K\xi =&\,\sum _{I=0}^{K-1}\left[ v*\nabla ^I\text { {Riem}}[G]*\underline{\nabla }^{L-I-1}\xi +\nabla ^{I}\text { {Riem}}[G]*\underline{\nabla }^{L-I-2}\xi \right] \\&\,+\langle \text {lower order nonlinear terms containing }v,\text { {Riem}}[G],\xi \rangle \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DL,Kξ-∇_vertL-K∇_horKξ=∑I=0K-1v∗∇IRiem[G]∗∇_L-I-1ξ+∇IRiem[G]∗∇_L-I-2ξ+⟨lower order nonlinear terms containingv,Riem[G],ξ⟩,where we recall \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text { {Riem}}[G]=\text { {Ric}}[G]*G$$\end{document}Riem[G]=Ric[G]∗G. Thus, the product rule and the a priori estimates (4.3e), (4.6a) and (4.16) yield the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\,\left\lvert \Vert \mathcal {D}_{L,K}(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert ^2_{L^2_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M)}-\Vert \underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^K(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert ^2_{L^2_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M)}\right\rvert \\&\quad \lesssim \,a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\Vert f-f_{FLRW}\Vert ^2_{H^{L-1}_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M)}+\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\mathfrak {E}^{(\le K-1)}(\text { {Ric}},\cdot )\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖DL,K(f-fFLRW)‖L1,G_02(T∗M)2-‖∇_vertL-K∇_horK(f-fFLRW)‖L1,G_02(T∗M)2≲a-cε‖f-fFLRW‖H1,G_0L-1(T∗M)2+εa-cεE(≤K-1)(Ric,·).Furthermore, after applying the triangle inequality to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^K(f-f_{FLRW})\Vert _{L^2_{1,\underline{G}_0}(T^*M)}$$\end{document}‖∇_vertL-K∇_horK(f-fFLRW)‖L1,G_02(T∗M), we can estimate the reference terms with (4.11a). (4.26a) then follows by iterating over iteratively replacing mixed terms on the right hand side until only Vlasov energy terms remain.

For (4.26b), we largely proceed as above replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f-f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}f-fFLRW with f. However, whenever we encounter a term with only one horizontal derivative by commuting, we replace f with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f-f_{FLRW})+f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}(f-fFLRW)+fFLRW. For the former, we simply use (4.26a). Regarding the latter, the analogous bounds hold as for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{L,1}f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}DL,1fFLRW as for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-1}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}∇_vertL-1∇_horfFLRW in (4.11a), except for incurring metric terms up to second order since taking vertical derivatives causes metric terms to occur at order zero that need to be differentiated: For example, one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\underline{\nabla }_{hor}\underline{\nabla }_{vert}f_{FLRW}=\underline{\nabla }_{hor}(\mathcal {F}^\prime (|v|_\gamma ^2)*v*\gamma )\\&\quad =v*\left( \underline{\nabla }_{hor}\mathcal {F}^\prime (|v|_\gamma ^2)*\gamma ^{-1}+\mathcal {F}^\prime (|v|_\gamma ^2)*\nabla (G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1})\right) \,.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇_hor∇_vertfFLRW=∇_hor(F′(|v|γ2)∗v∗γ)=v∗∇_horF′(|v|γ2)∗γ-1+F′(|v|γ2)∗∇(G-1-γ-1).Note that, since we only need to apply (4.11a) when only one horizontal derivative occurs, we do not incur higher order metric errors in (4.26b). □ □

Since Lemma 4.13 allows us to arrange covariant derivatives in arbitrary order up to curvature errors, this will imply that Vlasov energies are coercive up to lower order metric errors, along with bounds on the reference distribution function to control the purely vertical case. We will use this in Corollary 7.11 to improve bounds on Sobolev norms containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f-f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}f-fFLRW. To then improve the bootstrap assumptions, we need to show that these sufficiently control the energy momentum tensor, which is ensured by the following lemma:

Lemma 4.14

(Energy bounds for Vlasov energy-momentum tensor components). Proof

Note that we can apply Jensen’s inequality on continuous functions ξ ξ on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M$$\end{document}T∗M that have compact support after renormalising, i.e., for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(\textrm{supp}\xi )=\int _{\textrm{supp}\xi }\mu _G^{-1}dv$$\end{document}V(suppξ)=∫suppξμG-1dv,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _M\left( \int _{T^*_\cdot M}v^0\xi {\mu _G^{-1}}dv\right) ^2{\text { {vol}}}_{G}=&\int _MV(\textrm{supp}\xi )^2\left( V(\textrm{supp}\xi )^{-1}\int _{\textrm{supp}\xi }v^0\xi \,\mu _G^{-1}\,dv\right) ^2\,{\text { {vol}}}_{G}\\ \le&\,\int _{T^*M}V(\textrm{supp}\xi )(v^0)^2\xi ^2\,{\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫M∫T·∗Mv0ξμG-1dv2volG=∫MV(suppξ)2V(suppξ)-1∫suppξv0ξμG-1dv2volG≤∫T∗MV(suppξ)(v0)2ξ2volG_,and that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} V(\textrm{supp}\xi )\lesssim \mathcal {P}^3\lesssim a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V(suppξ)≲P3≲a-cεusing (4.6a). Thus, recalling (4.22), we compute for the energy density:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \rho ^{Vl}-{\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl}\Vert ^2_{L^2_G(M_t)}\\&\quad \lesssim \int _{M_t}\left( \int _{\textrm{supp}(f-f_{FLRW})(t,x,\cdot )}\left[ |f-f_{FLRW}|+|G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}|_G\right] v^0\,\mu _G^{-1}dv\right) ^2\,{\text { {vol}}}_{G}\\&\quad \lesssim a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\int _{T^*M_t}(v^0)^2\left[ |f-f_{FLRW}|^2+|G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}|_G^2\right] {\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\\&\quad \lesssim a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\left( \mathfrak {E}^{(0)}_{1,0}(f,t)+a(t)^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\Vert G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}\Vert ^2_{L^2_G(M_t)}\right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ρVl-ρFLRWVl‖LG2(Mt)2≲∫Mt∫supp(f-fFLRW)(t,x,·)|f-fFLRW|+|G-1-γ-1|Gv0μG-1dv2volG≲a(t)-cε∫T∗Mt(v0)2|f-fFLRW|2+|G-1-γ-1|G2volG_≲a(t)-cεE1,0(0)(f,t)+a(t)-cε‖G-1-γ-1‖LG2(Mt)2.The computations for the pressure are fully analogous, proving (4.27a) after updating constants. By (2.14), we can express \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\jmath ^{Vl}$$\end{document}ȷVl as a weighted integral over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f-f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}f-fFLRW, thus avoiding the metric error term. Regarding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^{Vl,\parallel }$$\end{document}SVl,‖, one can expand the term as in the proof of Corollary 4.10 to prove (4.27d) for L=0L=0. Estimates for Spacetime and Scalar Field Variables

All of the estimates in this and the following section hold for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0]. Furthermore, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \gg \sigma >0$$\end{document}ω≫σ>0 be a constant that is suitably small: For example, if σ σ is chosen small enough that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}<c\sigma <\frac{1}{1000}$$\end{document}cε18<cσ<11000 holds throughout the proof, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =\frac{1}{100}$$\end{document}ω=1100 is an admissible choice, but this choice is far from optimal. In general, ω ω only needs to be small compared to the order of derivatives used in our estimates, but large compared to σ σ.

Lapse energy estimates

In this section, we collect estimates for the lapse that follow from (2.21f)–(2.21g). In particular, in contrast to the estimates in [FU25, Section 5], we will also prove estimates for odd order lapse energies, since these will be necessary to control high order lapse terms.

Definition 5.1

(Elliptic operators). 5.1a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}\zeta =&\,a^4\Delta \zeta -h\cdot \zeta ,&h=&-3\kappa a^4+12\pi C^2+12\pi a^2\left( {\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl}+{\mathfrak {p}}_{FLRW}^{Vl}\right) +H\,,\nonumber \\ & H=&\,\langle \Sigma ,\Sigma \rangle _G+8\pi \Psi ^2+16\pi C\Psi \nonumber \\ & &\quad +4\pi a^2\left( \rho ^{Vl}-{\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl}+3\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}-3{\mathfrak {p}}_{FLRW}^{Vl}\right) \,,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lζ=a4Δζ-h·ζ,h=-3κa4+12πC2+12πa2ρFLRWVl+pFLRWVl+H,H=⟨Σ,Σ⟩G+8πΨ2+16πCΨ+4πa2ρVl-ρFLRWVl+3pVl-3pFLRWVl,5.1b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\mathcal {L}}\zeta =&\,a^4\Delta \zeta -\tilde{h}\cdot \zeta ,&\quad \tilde{h}=&-3\kappa a^4+12\pi C^2+12\pi a^2({\mathfrak {p}}_{FLRW}^{Vl}+{\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl})+\tilde{H}\,\nonumber \\ & \tilde{H}=&\,a^4\left[ R[G]-6\kappa -8\pi |\nabla \phi |^2_G\right] \nonumber \\ & &\quad +12\pi a^2\left[ (\mathfrak {p}^{Vl}-{\mathfrak {p}}_{FLRW}^{Vl})-(\rho ^{Vl}-{\rho }_{FLRW}^{Vl})\right] \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L~ζ=a4Δζ-h~·ζ,h~=-3κa4+12πC2+12πa2(pFLRWVl+ρFLRWVl)+H~H~=a4R[G]-6κ-8π|∇ϕ|G2+12πa2(pVl-pFLRWVl)-(ρVl-ρFLRWVl).

Proof

This proof now proceeds as in the pure scalar field setting (see [FU25, Corollary 5.5, 5.8]): We commute (5.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta ^l$$\end{document}Δl and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \Delta ^l$$\end{document}∇Δl for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l=\lfloor \frac{L}{2}\rfloor =0,\dots ,9$$\end{document}l=⌊L2⌋=0,⋯,9 and apply the elliptic estimates from Lemma 5.2 and 5.3 respectively. For the estimates with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L, the right hand sides at order L are of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{I=1}^{L-1}\nabla ^IH*\nabla ^{L-I}N+(N+1){\left\{ \begin{array}{ll}\Delta ^\frac{L}{2}H & L\text { even}\\ \nabla \Delta ^{\lfloor \frac{L}{2}\rfloor }H& L\text { odd}\end{array}\right. }\,.$$\end{document}∑I=1L-1∇IH∗∇L-IN+(N+1)ΔL2HLeven∇Δ⌊L2⌋HLodd.Again, the only changes occur when considering Vlasov terms: At high order, we apply Lemma 4.14 to estimate the density and pressure terms by Vlasov energies up to curvature errors. Conversely, low order Vlasov terms are bounded using (4.21). Regarding the remaining quantities, we note that [FU25, Lemma 4.5] fully extends to our setting, allowing us to move between Sobolev norms and energies up to curvature errors at high orders. □ □

Energy and norm estimates for the scalar field and remaining spacetime variables

In this section, we collect individual energy and Sobolev norm estimates for the shear as well as scalar field and metric variables. Compared to the estimates in [FU25, Section 6] and their respective proofs, there are two differences we need to account for: Firstly, we now consider arbitrary (constant) spatial sectional curvature instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =-\frac{1}{9}$$\end{document}κ=-19, which leads to a slightly more refined argument being necessary to prove Lemma 5.5. Secondly, Vlasov matter now enters the respective equations and the above lapse estimates. Beyond needing to estimate Vlasov matter terms where they occur, the fact that Vlasov matter enters into the elliptic lapse estimate (5.6c) leads to a linear coupling between scalar field and Vlasov matter. To deal with this, we need to introduce an additional time-scaling hierarchy in the total energy along with a hierarchy based on \varepsilon ε-scaling, see Definition (7.4). To this end, we additionally derive time-scaled estimates along similar lines as their unscaled analogues, and often distribute both types of weights in anticipation of the total energies in Definition (7.4). The proofs of the latter otherwise will follow by the same arguments as in the pure scalar field case, so we will often only focus on how these changes are accounted for in the respective proofs.

We will need an additional norm bound to obtain optimal control for the scalar field:

Lemma 5.6

(Norm bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \phi $$\end{document}∇ϕ).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \nabla \phi \Vert _{H^l_G(M)}\lesssim (1+\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }})\Vert \Sigma \Vert _{H^{l+1}_G(M)}+\sqrt{\varepsilon } a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\Vert \Psi \Vert _{H^l_G(M)}+(1+\varepsilon a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }})\Vert \jmath ^{Vl}\Vert _{H^l_G(M)}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∇ϕ‖HGl(M)≲(1+εa-cε)‖Σ‖HGl+1(M)+εa-cε‖Ψ‖HGl(M)+(1+εa-cε)‖ȷVl‖HGl(M).

Proof

The momentum constraint (2.21i) leads to the following expression after rearranging and using (4.1a) to ensure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi +C>\frac{C}{2}$$\end{document}Ψ+C>C2:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\nabla ^l\nabla \phi |_G=&\,\left\lvert \nabla ^l\left( \frac{\frac{1}{8\pi }\text { {div}}_G\Sigma +\jmath ^{Vl}}{\Psi +C}\right) \right\rvert _G\\ \lesssim&\,|\nabla ^{l+1}\Sigma |_G+|\nabla ^l\jmath ^{Vl}|_G+\sum _{I_1+I_2=l-1}\left( |\nabla ^{I_1+1}\Sigma |_G+|\nabla ^{I_1}\jmath ^{Vl}|_G\right) |\nabla ^{I_2+1}\Psi |_G\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|∇l∇ϕ|G=∇l18πdivGΣ+ȷVlΨ+CG≲|∇l+1Σ|G+|∇lȷVl|G+∑I1+I2=l-1|∇I1+1Σ|G+|∇I1ȷVl|G|∇I2+1Ψ|G.The statement then follows by applying the Young inequality with (4.3b), (4.21) and (4.1a). □ □

For the remaining terms, recall that we can apply (2.24) to pull the covariant derivative past the respective momentum integrals. By Lemma 2.7, any of the prefactors depending only on the momentum are uniformly bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^0$$\end{document}v0 up to constant and vanish when hit by a horizontal derivative. Keeping this in mind, we apply the product rule: When estimating high order Vlasov terms, we apply the Jensen inequality as in the proof of Lemma 4.14 to connect the respectice quantities to our Vlasov energies, and apply the low order bounds (4.3b) and (3.13a) for shear and lapse respectively. On the other hand, if most derivatives hit lapse or shear terms, we estimate the respective distribution function terms with (4.16) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}$$\end{document}a-cε, and simply apply (4.6a) to deal with the remaining integrals over functions of v. Finally, recall [FU25, Lemma 4.5] to bound Sobolev norms in Σ Σ and N to their respective energy terms up to curvature errors. □ □

Proof

The proof proceeds exactly as in the pure scalar field case (see [FU25, Lemma 6.6]), where we use Lemma 5.7 to bound the Vlasov matter terms, and apply Lemma 5.4 to bound lapse energies. □ □

Proof

Since (2.21a), (2.21d) and (2.21e) do not contain Vlasov matter variables, the proofs for (5.17b), (5.18b) and (5.19c)–(5.19f) are largely unchanged to those of [FU25, Lemma 6.11–6.14], where we apply Lemma 5.4 to estimate lapse energy terms. Regarding the scaled estimates, we consider (5.17a) for even l\ge 2l≥2; the other cases as well as (5.18a) are proven similarly: Commuting (2.21a) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta ^\frac{l}{2}$$\end{document}Δl2, we have the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\,-\partial _t\left( a(t)^\frac{\omega }{2}\Vert G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}\Vert _{H^l_G}^2\right) \\ \lesssim&\,a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+\frac{\omega }{2}}\left( \sqrt{\mathfrak {E}^{(\le l)}(\Sigma ,\cdot )}+\sqrt{\mathfrak {E}^{(\le l)}(N,\cdot )}+\sqrt{\mathfrak {E}^{(\le l-2)}(\text { {Ric}},\cdot )}\right) \Vert G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}\Vert _{H^l_G}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-∂ta(t)ω2‖G-1-γ-1‖HGl2≲a-3-cε+ω2E(≤l)(Σ,·)+E(≤l)(N,·)+E(≤l-2)(Ric,·)‖G-1-γ-1‖HGl.We apply the Young inequality to, for example, the shear term as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+\frac{\omega }{2}}\sqrt{\mathfrak {E}^{(\le l)}(\Sigma ,\cdot )}\Vert G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}\Vert _{H^l_G}\lesssim a^{-3+\frac{\omega }{8}}\cdot a^\frac{\omega }{2}\Vert G^{-1}-\gamma ^{-1}\Vert _{H^l_G}^2 +a^{-3+\frac{\omega }{8}-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\,\cdot a^\frac{\omega }{4}\mathfrak {E}^{(\le l)}(\Sigma ,\cdot )\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a-3-cε+ω2E(≤l)(Σ,·)‖G-1-γ-1‖HGl≲a-3+ω8·aω2‖G-1-γ-1‖HGl2+a-3+ω8-cε·aω4E(≤l)(Σ,·).The statement then follows by inserting Lemma 5.4, applying the Young inequality similarly to the other terms and integrating. From comparing (2.21b) to (2.21a), one quickly observes that the analogous bound for the inverse metric follows identically.

Finally, (5.19g) is a direct consequence of applying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta ^\frac{l}{2}$$\end{document}Δl2 (resp. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \Delta ^\frac{l-1}{2}$$\end{document}∇Δl-12) to the constraint equation (2.21j), rearranging and applying the a priori estimates (4.1b), (4.3b), (4.3d), (4.1a) and (4.3a). In particular, regarding the Vlasov terms, one uses (4.27d) and (4.27a) to connect their norms to our energy quantities. For the remaining terms, we use estimates as in [FU25, Lemma 4.5], along with the previously mentioned a priori estimates, to convert Sobolev norms into energies up to curvature errors. □ □

Vlasov Energy Estimates

In this section, we collect the necessary energy estimates to obtain the bootstrap improvements in the following section. In particular, for each derivative order and each number of horizontal derivatives in the energy, we prove both a time-scaled estimate, which are all combined in Lemma 7.3 to obtain bootstrap improvements for the spacetime and scalar field variables, as well as non-scaled estimates that lose smallness, which we use to improve the bootstrap assumptions on Vlasov matter. Regarding the time-scaled estimates, we scale by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\omega $$\end{document}aω and powers thereof to indicate scaling necessary within the hierarchy of vertical and horizontal derivatives at a given order (foreshadowing (7.2)), and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^\beta $$\end{document}aβ to indicate additional scaling necessary for the total energy at odd derivative orders (foreshadowing the final line of (7.5c)).

Preliminaries

First, we collect some tools that will be used throughout:

Remark 6.1

(Integrating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Next, we collect the core estimate that ensures the transport equation almost conserves Vlasov energies:

Lemma 6.2

(Energy mechanism for the Vlasov equation). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \ge 0$$\end{document}μ≥0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le L\le 19$$\end{document}0≤L≤19. Then, the following holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{T^*M_t}\langle v\rangle ^{2\mu }\langle \boldsymbol{X}\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L}(f-f_{FLRW}),\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L}(f-f_{FLRW})\rangle _{\underline{G}_0}{\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma }\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{\mu ,0}(f,\cdot )\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T∗Mt⟨v⟩2μ⟨X∇_vertL(f-fFLRW),∇_vertL(f-fFLRW)⟩G_0volG_≲εa1-cσEμ,0(L)(f,·).Similarly, if one additionally has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le K\le L$$\end{document}1≤K≤L, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{T^*M_t}\langle v\rangle ^{2\mu }\langle \boldsymbol{X}\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^Kf,\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^Kf\rangle _{\underline{G}_0}{\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon a^{1-c\sigma }\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{\mu ,K}(f,\cdot )\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T∗Mt⟨v⟩2μ⟨X∇_vertL-K∇_horKf,∇_vertL-K∇_horKf⟩G_0volG_≲εa1-cσEμ,K(L)(f,·).

Proof

Both estimates are proved identically, so we only write out the proof for the second. Writing out \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}$$\end{document}X (see (2.20b)), the left hand side takes the following form:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{T^*M_t}\langle v\rangle _G^{2\mu }(v^0)^{2(L-K)}\left\langle \left[ -a^{-1}\frac{v^{\sharp j}}{v^0}(N+1)\underline{\nabla }_j+a^{-1}v^0\nabla _jN\,\textbf{B}^j\right] \underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^Kf,\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^Kf\right\rangle _{\underline{G}}\,{\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T∗Mt⟨v⟩G2μ(v0)2(L-K)-a-1v♯jv0(N+1)∇_j+a-1v0∇jNBj∇_vertL-K∇_horKf,∇_vertL-K∇_horKfG_volG_.Regarding the first term, we integrate by parts with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_j$$\end{document}∇_j, noting that the horizontal covariant derivative of any momentum weight vanishes. For the remaining term, we use Fubini’s theorem to replace the integral by one over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _M\int _{T^*_xM_t}$$\end{document}∫M∫Tx∗Mt and then integrate by parts in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}$$\end{document}B. This leads to the following after rearranging and applying (2.25c):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{T^*M_t}&\,\langle v\rangle _G^{2\mu }\langle \boldsymbol{X}\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L}(f-f_{FLRW}),\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L}(f-f_{FLRW})\rangle _{\underline{G}_0}{\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\\ \!=\!\int _{T^*M_t}&\,a^{\!-\!1}\langle v\rangle ^{2\mu }_G\,\left( v^{\sharp j}\nabla _jN\right) \,v^0\left( 2\mu \langle v\rangle _G^{\!-\!2}\!+\!2(L\!-\!K)(v^0)^{\!-\!2}\right) |\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L\!-\!K}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^Kf|_{\underline{G}_0}^2\,{\text { {vol}}}_{\underline{G}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫T∗Mt⟨v⟩G2μ⟨X∇_vertL(f-fFLRW),∇_vertL(f-fFLRW)⟩G_0volG_=∫T∗Mta-1⟨v⟩G2μv♯j∇jNv02μ⟨v⟩G-2+2(L-K)(v0)-2|∇_vertL-K∇_horKf|G_02volG_.The statement then follows using (3.14). □ □

To be able to apply the above identity, we need to commute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t$$\end{document}∂t past the respective covariant Sasaki derivatives, apply (2.20a), and then commute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}$$\end{document}X back past them. We have already collected error term formulas for the former in Lemma 4.12, and now collect schematic formulas for the latter:

Proof

We first consider the purely vertical case (6.1a), then the purely horizontal case (6.4a), and then briefly (6.3a) as a combination of both cases before discussing the respective error bounds.

For (6.1a), we first replace f with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(f-f_{FLRW})+f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}(f-fFLRW)+fFLRW, which allows us to collect all reference error terms in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^L\boldsymbol{X}f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}∇_vertLXfFLRW. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}_i$$\end{document}Bi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{i+3}$$\end{document}∇_i+3 coincide when acting on purely vertical tensors (see (2.17)) and vertical frame derivatives commute, the only error terms generated from the vertical term in computing (6.1a) are down to applying the product rule to momentum prefactors. Regarding the horizontal term in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}$$\end{document}X, we additionally need to apply (4.24a) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_2=0$$\end{document}M2=0, which yields the term in the second line of (6.1a) as the leading error term, while all others are absorbed into the error terms. In particular, we note that this commutation only generates terms of the type in the second line of (6.1a), where we can always pull the horizontal derivative back to the front up to error terms as in Lemma 4.13 that are absorbed into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {F}_{L,0}$$\end{document}FL,0. We also note that, whenever vertical derivatives hit v-prefactors, both a power of v and a derivative are lost, and thus the momentum scaling of lower order error terms always precisely matches that of the respective \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{G}_0$$\end{document}G_0-norm, up to estimating v by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^0$$\end{document}v0 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\cdot |_G$$\end{document}|·|G.

For (6.4a), we need to ensure that, after taking horizontal derivatives of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}f$$\end{document}Xf and applying the product rule, we can still express all terms covariantly despite Christoffel symbols occuring explicitly in the commutator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\textbf{A}_i,\textbf{B}^j]f=-\Gamma [G]^j_{il}\textbf{B}^lf$$\end{document}[Ai,Bj]f=-Γ[G]iljBlf, see (2.26b). To this end, we commute horizontal derivatives past the vertical part of the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}$$\end{document}X as follows.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{\nabla }_i\left[ v^0\nabla _jN\textbf{B}^jf\right] =&\,v^0\textbf{A}_j\left[ \nabla _jN\textbf{B}^jf\right] =\,v^0\left[ \partial _i\nabla _jN\,\textbf{B}^jf+\nabla _jN\,\textbf{A}_i\textbf{B}^jf\right] \\ =&\,v^0\left[ \partial _i\nabla _jN\,\textbf{B}^jf+\nabla _jN\,\left( \textbf{B}^j\textbf{A}_if-\Gamma [G]^j_{il}\textbf{B}^lf\right) \right] \\ =&\,v^0\left[ \nabla _i\nabla _jN\,\textbf{B}^jf+\nabla _jN\textbf{B}^j\underline{\nabla }_if\right] \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇_iv0∇jNBjf=v0Aj∇jNBjf=v0∂i∇jNBjf+∇jNAiBjf=v0∂i∇jNBjf+∇jNBjAif-Γ[G]iljBlf=v0∇i∇jNBjf+∇jNBj∇_if.At higher orders, one additionally incurs terms of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v*\text { {Riem}}[G]$$\end{document}v∗Riem[G] from connection coefficients with two horizontal indices. Thus, repeating this argument, we obtain the following schematic representation:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\,[\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^L,v^0\nabla _jN\,\textbf{B}^jf]f\\ =&\,\sum _{K_N+K_f=L,\,K_f\ne L}v*\nabla ^{K_N+1}N*\textbf{B}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^{K_f}f\\&\,+\sum _{K_\text { {Ric}}+K_N+K_f=L-2}v*\nabla ^{K_\text { {Ric}}}\text { {Ric}}[G]*\nabla ^{K_N+1}N*\textbf{B}\underline{\nabla }_{hor}^{K_f}f\\&\,+\langle \text {low order nonlinear error terms with multiple curvature terms}\rangle \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[∇_horL,v0∇jNBjf]f=∑KN+Kf=L,Kf≠Lv∗∇KN+1N∗B∇_horff+∑KRic+KN+Kf=L-2v∗∇KRicRic[G]∗∇KN+1N∗B∇_horff+⟨low order nonlinear error terms with multiple curvature terms⟩.Regarding the horizontal term in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}f$$\end{document}Xf, we apply (4.24a) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1=0, M_2=L$$\end{document}M1=0,M2=L, again noting that we can rearrange the covariant derivatives up to error terms into the order presented, and can deal with the error terms as in Lemma 4.13. Finally, (6.3a) follows from combining the ideas from both of these proofs, commuting with horizontal derivative terms as in the purely vertical case and noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}$$\end{document}B and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_{vert}^{L-K}$$\end{document}∇_vertL-K commute.

The error bounds (6.4b), (6.3b) and (6.1b) simply follow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2-L^\infty -$$\end{document}L2-L∞-estimating the remaining terms and using (4.16) for low order Vlasov terms, (4.3e) for the curvature terms and (4.6a) to control momentum terms as well as the lapse bootstrap assumption from (3.13a). In particular, we note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}f$$\end{document}Bf and higher order vertical derivatives of it are only bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}$$\end{document}a-cε, leading to the lapse energies in the second line of (6.4b). Furthermore, we apply (4.26b) to order the derivatives in the Vlasov error terms as needed, and note that the leading curvature terms arise from this procedure.

Finally, for (6.2), we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^0$$\end{document}v0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle v\rangle _G$$\end{document}⟨v⟩G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{B}f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}BfFLRW are bounded by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}$$\end{document}a-cε on the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{FLRW}$$\end{document}fFLRW since the latter has bounded support with respect to γ γ and one has (4.3c). Using (4.12) for the horizontal term in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{X}$$\end{document}X then directly leads to the stated bound.

□ □

Vlasov energy estimates

Recalling our conventions on t and ω ω from the start of this section, we now start by proving the Vlasov integral energy estimate at order 0:

Lemma 6.4

(Zero order Vlasov energy estimates). Lemma 6.6

For the scaled version, the main point is that one needs to be careful to ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K-1}(f,\cdot )$$\end{document}E1,K-1(L)(f,·) terms do not enter at a power worse than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-3}$$\end{document}a-3, which is the case in the unscaled version. To this end, we distribute the scaling as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+(K+1){\omega }}\sqrt{\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,\cdot )}\sqrt{\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K-1}(f,\cdot )}=a^{-3+\frac{\omega }{2}}\sqrt{a^{(K+1)\omega }\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,\cdot )}\sqrt{a^{K\omega -2c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,\cdot )}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a-3-cε+(K+1)ωE1,K(L)(f,·)E1,K-1(L)(f,·)=a-3+ω2a(K+1)ωE1,K(L)(f,·)aKω-2cεE1,K(L)(f,·).Then, we apply the Young inequality and update c. We note that one never encounters more than K horizontal derivatives in the error terms and thus can never lose scaling. For the non-Vlasov energies, we distribute scaling as in the previous arguments. Again, replacing β =0β=0 with β =4β=4 goes through identically. □ □

Lemma 6.7

(Horizontal Vlasov energy estimates). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \{0,4\}$$\end{document}β∈{0,4} and L\ge 2L≥2. \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a(t)^{\beta +(L+1)\omega }&\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,L}(f,t)\lesssim \varepsilon ^4+\int _t^{t_0}\left( a(s)^{-3+\frac{\omega }{2}}+\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8} a(s)^{-3}\right) a(s)^{\beta +(L+1)\omega }\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,L}(f,s)\,ds\nonumber \\&\,+\int _t^{t_0}\varepsilon a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+\frac{\omega }{2}}\cdot a(s)^{\beta +L{\omega }}\mathfrak {E}^{(\le L)}_{1,\le L-1}(f,s)\,ds\nonumber \\&\,+\int _t^{t_0}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+\omega }\cdot a(s)^\beta \left( a(s)^\frac{\omega }{4}\mathfrak {E}^{(\le L)}(\Sigma ,s)+\mathfrak {E}^{(\le L)}(\phi ,s) \right) \,ds\nonumber \\&\,+\int _t^{t_0}a(s)^{-3-c\sqrt{\varepsilon }+\frac{(2L-1){\omega }}{2}}\cdot a(s)^{4+\beta +\omega Proof

The proof of these estimates is analogous to that of Lemma 6.6 since (6.4a) is just a simpler version of (6.3a), and the commutator formula (4.24b) is also used for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_1=0$$\end{document}M1=0 in a similar fashion to the previous proof. As a difference in estimation, we note that the high order curvature term present in (6.4a) is now of order L-1L-1, and thus needs to be thought of as being of order L+1L+1 in the metric, i.e., of higher order than the Vlasov energy itself. Consequently, the resulting energy controlling this term needs to be additionally scaled to allow the argument to close, which makes the resulting energy term borderline. □ □

Improving the Bootstrap Assumptions

In this section, we combine the energy estimates from Sects. 5, and 6 to improve the bootstrap assumption (3.13a)—recall that (3.13b) is already improved due to (4.16). To this end, we first derive improved energy bounds for the spacetime and scalar field variables and then use these to derive bounds for Vlasov energies. We then translate these bounds to Sobolev norm bounds, and finally to bounds on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C.

In the following, we again assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0] and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \gg \sigma $$\end{document}ω≫σ is a suitably small constant, see also the start of Section 5.

To deal with linear borderline terms in the energy estimates for the shear (see Lemma 5.9) and Bel-Robinson variables (see Lemma 5.8), we need to extend [FU25, Lemma 7.1] to ensure that the error terms essentially have favourable sign even for flat and spherical spatial geometry:

Lemma 7.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\in 2\mathbb {N}$$\end{document}L∈2N. Then, the following estimate is satisfied:7.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\,\int _M\left[ 16\pi C^2a^{-3}(N+1)\langle \Delta ^\frac{L}{2}\mathcal {E},\Delta ^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma \rangle _G+8\pi C^2\frac{\dot{a}}{a}(N+1)|\Delta ^\frac{L}{2}\Sigma |_G^2+6\frac{\dot{a}}{a}(N+1)|\Delta ^\frac{L}{2}\mathcal {E}|_G^2\right] {\text { {vol}}}_{G}\nonumber \\&{\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \ge 0 & \kappa \le 0\\ \gtrsim -a^{-1}\left[ 4\pi C^2\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}(\Sigma ,\cdot )+\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}(W,\cdot )\right] & \kappa >0 \end{array}\right. }\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫M16πC2a-3(N+1)⟨ΔL2E,ΔL2Σ⟩G+8πC2a˙a(N+1)|ΔL2Σ|G2+6a˙a(N+1)|ΔL2E|G2volG≥0κ≤0≳-a-14πC2E(L)(Σ,·)+E(L)(W,·)κ>0.

Proof

Firstly, the bootstrap assumption (3.14) implies N+1>0N+1>0 for \varepsilon >0ε>0 small enough. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \le 0$$\end{document}κ≤0, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-3}\le \frac{3}{4\pi C^2}\frac{\dot{a}}{a}$$\end{document}a-3≤34πC2a˙a by (2.3a) since the Vlasov density is nonnegative. Then, the statement follows directly from this and the Young inequality as in [FU25, Lemma 7.1]. Else, one has\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a^{-3}\le \frac{3}{4\pi C^2}\frac{\sqrt{\dot{a}^2+\kappa }}{a}\le \frac{3}{4\pi C^2}\left( \frac{\dot{a}}{a}+\sqrt{\kappa }a^{-1}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}a-3≤34πC2a˙2+κa≤34πC2a˙a+κa-1and thus the integrand on the left hand side in (7.1) is bounded from below by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \gtrsim -a^{-1}|\Delta ^{\frac{L}{2}}\Sigma |_G|\Delta ^\frac{L}{2}\mathcal {E}|_G\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}≳-a-1|ΔL2Σ|G|ΔL2E|G,implying the statement. □ □

To obtain bounds on the spacetime and scalar field energies, we need to combine Vlasov energies at order L with appropriate scaling as follows:

Definition 7.2

(Total scaled Vlasov energies). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le L\le 19$$\end{document}0≤L≤19, we define7.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{total,Vl}=\sum _{K=0}^La^{(K+1)\omega }\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,\cdot )\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Etotal,Vl(L)=∑K=0La(K+1)ωE1,K(L)(f,·).

Recalling the discussion in Sect. 1.4.2, we would like to construct a total energy as in [FU25] that admits a strong bound via the Gronwall lemma. To this end, we essentially take the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{quiesc}$$\end{document}Equiesc(L) that describes the quiescent formalism as in [FU25] and add the total Vlasov energy to it, which forces us to add further scaled terms to the total energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{total}$$\end{document}Etotal(L). Before explaining the precise rationale behind these terms, we introduce these energies.

Remark 7.5

(On the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{total}$$\end{document}Etotal(L)) Essentially, the total energy can be split into three components—\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{quiesc}$$\end{document}Equiesc(L) which describes the core quiescent mechanism as in [FU25], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{total,Vl}$$\end{document}Etotal,Vl(L) which is a natural energy for Vlasov matter in near-FLRW regimes, and various scaled energies controlling components of the spacetime metric. We briefly explain why the latter needs to be introduced when one attempts to combine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{quiesc}$$\end{document}Equiesc(L) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{total,Vl}$$\end{document}Etotal,Vl(L) into a total energy that admits a useful Gronwall estimate:

First, we notice that Vlasov energies occur linearly in energy estimates for the scalar field, see Lemma 5.5, and also vice versa, see (7.4). Thus, we do not gain smallness in terms of \varepsilon ε, and the Vlasov energy must enter the total energy without \varepsilon ε-weights. Second, Vlasov matter also linearly couples into the geometric evolution equations for the shear and for Bel-Robinson variables, see Lemmas 5.14 and 5.8, and vice versa, see (7.4). However, to obtain a closed energy estimate for the quiescent components, one needs to introduce an \varepsilon ε-weight in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{quiesc}$$\end{document}Equiesc(L) as in [FU25]. On the other hand, geometric energies occur linearly in (7.4). Thus, to obtain closed estimates, we must introduce geometric energies that are not weighted by \varepsilon ε. To nevertheless surpress linear scalar field terms of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^{-1}$$\end{document}t-1 in the geometric estimates, we must then introduce scale factor weights on these terms, which must be weaker than those on Vlasov matter. This then extends to Ricci curvature terms as well as low order geometric error terms in the Vlasov equation, which must both exhibit stronger scaling than the second fundamental form, which occurs linearly at order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t^{-1}$$\end{document}t-1 in the evolution equations of either. For the second fundamental form at order L+1L+1, the additional scale factor weight must be stronger than the one at order L so that, after applying Lemma 5.10, all resulting terms can be absorbed into the left hand side if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a(t_0)$$\end{document}a(t0) is sufficiently small. The curvature term at order L-1L-1 must, in turn, be scaled more strongly than the second fundamental form at order L+1L+1 as before, and the top order curvature term must be similarly strongly weighted so that one can absorb terms resulting from (5.19g) into the left hand side.

Proposition 7.6

(Total energy bound). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L\in 2\mathbb {N}, L\le 18$$\end{document}L∈2N,L≤18, the following holds:7.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{total}\lesssim \varepsilon ^4a^{-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Etotal(L)≲ε4a-cε18.

(Sobolev norm estimates for the solution variables). 7.9a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Further, one has the following high order bounds: 7.10a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a^4\mathfrak {E}^{(17)}(\text { {Ric}},\cdot )\lesssim \varepsilon ^\frac{7}{2} a^{-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}\,,\end{aligned}$$\end{document}a4E(17)(Ric,·)≲ε72a-cε18,7.10b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Gamma -\hat{\Gamma }\Vert _{H^{17}_G}+a^4\Vert \Gamma -\hat{\Gamma }\Vert _{\dot{H}^{18}_G}\lesssim&\,\varepsilon ^\frac{7}{4} a^{-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Γ-Γ^‖HG17+a4‖Γ-Γ^‖H˙G18≲ε74a-cε18.

Proof

The first three bounds as well as the Ricci bounds follow directly from Proposition 7.6 using [FU25, Corollary 4.5] to switch bound the respective Sobolev norms up to curvature energies. To obtain the lapse bounds, we apply (5.6b) and (5.6c), where we observe that, for suitable ω ω, we can estimate all Vlasov energies using (7.8). The metric and Christoffel bounds then follow from (5.17b) and (5.18b). □ □

With these improvements in hand, we are now able to derive energy bounds for Vlasov matter that are not scaled:
