The shear and scalar field energies can be treated as before, and (7.9) leads to similar terms for curvature and metric error terms that are not at top order. At highest order (i.e., L=18L=18), we note that high order curvature terms that occur in (7.8) are appropriately scaled by (7.10). Altogether, (6.12) then leads to the following estimate:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,t)\lesssim \varepsilon ^4+\int _t^{t_0}\left( a(s)^{-1-c\sigma }+\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}a(s)^{-3}\right) \mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,K}(f,s)+\varepsilon ^\frac{7}{2}a(s)^{-1-\omega -c\sqrt{\varepsilon }}+\varepsilon ^\frac{29}{8}a(s)^{-3-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}\,ds, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E1,K(L)(f,t)≲ε4+∫t0a(s)-1-cσ+ε18a(s)-3E1,K(L)(f,s)+ε72a(s)-1-ω-cε+ε298a(s)-3-cε18ds,and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,\le K}(f,\cdot )\lesssim \varepsilon ^\frac{7}{2} a^{-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}$$\end{document}E1,≤K(L)(f,·)≲ε72a-cε18. Having now proven the bound up to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,\le L-1}(f,\cdot )$$\end{document}E1,≤L-1(L)(f,·) by iterating over K, we can apply the same argument to (6.16). Again, note that even at highest order, the metric and curvature bounds (7.9) and (7.10) are sufficient. This implies the stated bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {E}^{(L)}_{1,\le L}(f,\cdot )$$\end{document}E1,≤L(L)(f,·), completing the iteration step and thus proving (7.11). □ □

Now, we are in the position to improve the remaining bootstrap assumption (3.13a):

Corollary 7.10

(Bootstrap improvement). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C as in (3.2c) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (t_{Boot},t_0]$$\end{document}t∈(tBoot,t0], the following holds:7.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}(t)\lesssim \varepsilon ^\frac{7}{4} a(t)^{-c\varepsilon ^\frac{1}{8}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(t)≲ε74a(t)-cε18.

The Main Theorem

Remark 8.2

(On AVTD behaviour) (8.2) demonstrates that the evolution of metric and scalar field are asymptotically velocity term dominated (AVTD) in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(g,k,n,\partial _0\phi )$$\end{document}(g,k,n,∂0ϕ) exhibit asymptotic behaviour which, agrees at first order with the solution to the truncated Einstein Vlasov scalar-field system in CMC gauge in which we drop all spatial derivatives from (2.21a), (2.21c), (2.21f) and (2.21p). Note that, when considering the evolution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${k^i}_j$$\end{document}kij (or, equivalently, its tracefree part \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{k}^i}_j$$\end{document}k^ij) as well as the lapse equation, Vlasov matter terms are not truncated, leading to lower order terms in the VTD solution—as opposed to the pure scalar-field case, where the VTD solution is simply described by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^{-3}K_{Bang}$$\end{document}a-3KBang. Similarly truncating the rescaled Vlasov equation (2.20a) simply leads to the equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _tf_{VTD}=0$$\end{document}∂tfVTD=0, if one views the full Sasaki derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\nabla }_i$$\end{document}∇_i as the horizontal term to be truncated as the form of the isotropic solution suggests. Thus, the Vlasov distribution on the co-mass shell is also AVTD, but one loses control of the derived energy-momentum quantities since the leading order term is influenced by the level of inhomogeneity encoded in even the formal VTD solution via the asymptotics of the spatial metric. Thus, these asymptotics are in line with the critical behaviour of radiation fluids discussed in Sect. 1.3.2, since the corresponding macroscopic quantities degenerate towards the Big Bang, but at controllable rates.

The bound (8.4b) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^0_{1,\underline{\gamma }_0}(T^*M)$$\end{document}C1,γ_00(T∗M) then follows directly from above for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=t_0$$\end{document}t=t0 and s↓ 0s↓0. For higher orders, the argument is analogous, instead considering\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt}\left( \langle v\rangle _\gamma ^{L-K}\,\hat{\mathcal {D}}_{L,K}f\right) =\langle v\rangle _\gamma ^{L-K}\,\hat{\mathcal {D}}_{L,K}\left( \boldsymbol{X}f\right) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt⟨v⟩γL-KD^L,Kf=⟨v⟩γL-KD^L,KXf,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathcal {D}}_{L,K}$$\end{document}D^L,K denotes as combination of L-KL-K vertical and K horizontal covariant derivatives with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(T^*M,\underline{\gamma })$$\end{document}(T∗M,γ_). More precisely, it goes through as long as (8.1b) can be used to control derivatives of one order higher, i.e., the argument extends to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{10}_{1,{\underline{\gamma }}_0}(T^*M)$$\end{document}C1,γ_010(T∗M). In particular, note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t$$\end{document}∂t and covariant derivatives with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\nabla }}$$\end{document}∇_^ commute, and commutation errors between Sasaki metrics with respect to γ γ and G can be controlled sufficiently by (8.1a). The momentum support bounds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{Bang}$$\end{document}fBang are obtained by considering the limit of the support bounds with respect to γ γ in Lemma 4.6.

The remaining Vlasov matter bounds follow directly from (8.1a). Everything else is proven identically to [FU25, Theorem 8.2] and [RS18b, Theorem 5.1]—we note the convergence rates in (8.5) are weaker than in these results since the presence of Vlasov matter leads to a weaker convergence rate for the lapse (see (8.2a)). □ □

It was not necessary in the analysis above to assume that the momentum support of the initial perturbation was close to that of the FLRW solution, as long as their joint support was uniformly bounded and, in the massless case, bounded away from zero. However, if one additionally requires this, this property is preserved throughout the evolution.

Corollary 8.3

Consider the setting of Theorem 8.1 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{dist}_{\underline{\gamma }}$$\end{document}distγ_ denote the distance function on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^*M$$\end{document}T∗M induced by the Sasaki metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\gamma }$$\end{document}γ_. Additionally, the following analogue of (8.2f) holds and admits a limiting renormalised metric on the Big Bang hypersurface in the style used in [OGPR26].

Remark 8.5

(Interpreting the asymptotics on the mass shell) One might hope to obtain similar asymptotics for the Vlasov distribution on the mass shell. In particular, when interpreting the Vlasov distribution as a kinetic description of particle trajectories, the velocity of a particle is more naturally understood as a section of the mass shell, while the co-mass shell is the more natural choice from the Hamiltonian perspective. However, even on the level of the VTD solution, understanding the picture on the mass shell becomes difficult: Viewing the Vlasov equation (3.12) on the mass shell, it is convenient to view this equation in CMCTC gauge in expansion normalised momentum variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^i=a(t)^2q^i$$\end{document}wi=a(t)2qi, the characteristics of which are clearly preserved in the FLRW case, and to study\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f^{\sharp }_{\textrm{resc}}(t,x,w):=f^\sharp (t,x,a(t)^{-2}w). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fresc♯(t,x,w):=f♯(t,x,a(t)-2w).Truncating the resulting evolution equation in terms of these variables and denoting the lapse and shear of the VTD solution by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{VTD}$$\end{document}nVTD and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_{VTD}^\parallel $$\end{document}kVTD‖, one obtains the following:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _tf^\sharp _{VTD}=-2(n_{VTD}-1)\frac{\dot{a}}{a}w^j-2n_{VTD}{(k_{VTD}^\parallel )^j}_{i}w^i\partial _{w^j}f_{VTD}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tfVTD♯=-2(nVTD-1)a˙awj-2nVTD(kVTD‖)jiwi∂wjfVTD.For initial data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts}
